Sie kritisierten zudem die Sanktionen gegen Empfänger*innen, die sich nicht an die Vorgaben halten. Bei Redaktionsschluss diese Berichts waren die vorgeschlagenen Maßnahmen zur Bekämpfung der steigenden Kinderarmut und zur Begrenzung von Mieterhöhungen noch nicht verabschiedet.\nEin Anstieg der Lebenshaltungskosten, einschließlich der\nLebensmittelpreise\n, führte dazu, dass viele Menschen\nsich keinen angemessenen Lebensstandard mehr leisten\nkonnten. Haushalte mit Alleinerziehenden, die zu 88 Prozent von Frauen geführt werden, waren hiervon besonders betroffen.\nKlimawandel und politische Auswirkungen\nAls", "excerpt_sha256": "f02af1f24716dd3dd8f328cd1378e7ceb62dc14392af953eeb61f7166202c675", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0009-E012", "source_id": "SRC-60a3eb49fd3d4236", "url": "https://www.bundestag.de/dokumente/textarchiv/2023/kw38-de-onlinezugangsgesetz-965016", "title": "Kritik am Stand der Verwaltungsdigitalisierung", "publisher": "Deutscher Bundestag", "raw_sha256": "adc350cca41a0fac234b5b8bcfd7b40d87c7aa5b8e62639b4942e6365aaf1cb9", "fact_summary": "At the October 2023 hearing, experts reported that only 105 of the 575 services targeted for online access by the end of 2022 were online at the previous year-end and agreed that the original Online Access Act had missed its target; they also found the 2023 amendment insufficient, citing absent standards, timelines, cost clarity, independent monitoring, security rules, and a common technical model.", "question_ids": ["5B.6", "5B.7", "8B.5", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "2023 implementation assessment and hearing", "ruler_attribution": "Independent experts assessing the federal government's digital-administration programme", "limitations": ["The implementation shortfall began under the 2017 law and is not solely attributable to Scholz."], "start_unit": 33, "end_unit": 36, "locator": "extracted HTML block 33 through extracted HTML block 36", "exact_excerpt": "Kritik am Stand der Verwaltungsdigi­talisierung\nZeit:\nMontag, 9. Oktober 2023, 14 Uhr\nOrt:\nBerlin, Paul-Löbe-Haus, Sitzungssaal E 600\nSachverständige aus Wirtschaft, Wissenschaft, Zivilgesellschaft und Kommunalverbänden haben am\nMontag, 9. Oktober 2023,\ndie Forderung bekräftigt, die Digitalisierung der öffentlichen Verwaltung mit Nachdruck voranzutreiben. Dazu sei der vorliegende Entwurf einer Novelle des\nOnlinezugangsgesetzes\n(OZG) (\n20/8093\n(Dokument, öffnet ein neues Fenster)\n) zwar ein „Schritt in die richtige Richtung“, aber nach wie vor unzureichend, hieß es in einer Anhörung im Ausschuss für Inneres und Heimat.\nMit dem 2017 verabschiedeten Onlinezugangsgesetz hatten sich Bund und Länder die Aufgabe gestellt, den Bürgern bis Ende 2022 ein Paket von 575 staatlichen Dienstleistungen auf digitalem Wege zugänglich zu machen. Zum vergangenen Jahreswechsel waren davon indes nur 105 Angebote online geschaltet, mittlerweile sind es einige mehr. Dass das Gesetz sein Ziel bisher verfehlt hat, war Konsens unter den Sachverständigen. Umstritten war, ob die Einführung eines gesetzlichen Rechtsanspruchs auf digitale Verwaltungsleistungen, wie ihn die Unionsfraktion in einem Antrag (\n20/4313\n(Dokument, öffnet ein neues Fenster)\n) fordert, ein geeignetes Mittel der Beschleunigung sei.\nStaatliches Handeln im digitalen Zeitalter\nDer ehemalige Bevollmächtigte des Saarlandes für Innovation und Strategie,\nAmmar Alkassar\n, würdigte in der Anhörung das OZG „trotz aller Defizite“ als bisher umfangreichste Initiative, um staatliches Handeln auf das digitale Zeitalter einzustellen. Die jetzt vorliegende Novelle sei „positiv“, bleibe aber „hinter den Erfordernissen einer raschen Verwaltungsdigitalisierung“ zurück, sagte Alkassem. Es fehlten nicht nur „einige grundlegende Bausteine“, sondern ein „gemeinsames Zielbild“ im Sinne eines „plattformbasierten Ökosystems der öffentlichen Hand“. Zudem sei „Überkomplexität“ abzubauen: „Eine konsequente Umsetzung der Vewaltungsdigitalisierung erfordert einen echten Einschnitt“, mahnte Alkassem.\n„Nach fünf Jahren OZG ist Deutschland nicht dort, wo es in der Verwaltungsdigitalisierung sein sollte“, klagte\nGrudrun Aschenbrenner\nvon\nVorstand der Anstalt für kommunale Datenverarbeitung in Bayern\n\n. Sie wünsche sich „disruptive Änderungen“ und mehr Mut. In anderen Ländern gelte längst das Prinzip, Verwaltungsvorgänge nur noch digital abzuwickeln: „Was hält uns davon ab?“\nStandards, Zielsetzung und Kosten\nAls Vertreterin des\nDeutschen Städtetages\nwies\nUda Bastians\nauf die zunehmende Personalnot der öffentlichen Verwaltung bei wachsenden Anforderungen hin, die die Umstellung auf digitale Verfahren zu einem zwingenden Gebot werden ließen. Bis 2030 werde ein Drittel der derzeit Beschäftigten in den Ruhestand gehen. Zugleich drehe sich das Karussell der Gesetzesnovellen immer schneller. So sei allein das Aufenthaltsrecht seit 2015 nicht weniger als 59 Mal geändert worden: „Die Digitalisierung ist der Schlüssel, um das Land am Laufen zu halten.“ Gemessen daran bleibe der vorliegende Entwurf hinter den Erwartungen zurück. Es fehlten klare Festlegungen auf Standards, Zielsetzung und Kosten.\nFür den\nDeutschen Landkreistag\nwies\nAriane Berger\ndie Forderung nach einem Rechtsanspruch auf digitale Verwaltungsleistungen, der nach Vorstellung der Union 2025 in Kraft treten soll, als untauglich zurück. Die kommunale IT müsse für die Erfordernisse einer weitgehenden Digitalisierung „aufnahmefähig“ gemacht werden. Es bedürfe eines „Standardisierungprogramms“ für die Verwaltung.\nRechtsanspruch auf digitale Verwaltungsleistungen\n„Grundsätzlich begrüßenswert“, aber nicht ausreichend, um das „\nE-Government\n-Entwicklungsland“ in einen „digitalen Staat“ zu verwandeln, nannte\nJonas Botta\nvom\nDeutschen Forschungsinstitut für öffentliche Verwaltung in Speyer\nden vorliegenden Gesetzentwurf und empfahl, ihn „nachzuschärfen“. Die Novelle müsse, wie das bisherige OZG, erneut eine Frist für die Umsetzung enthalten. Es bedürfe klarer Kriterien, um den Realisierungsfortschritt durch eine unabhängige Stelle regelmäßig evaluieren zu lassen. Nicht zuletzt erklärte Botta einen Rechtsanspruch auf digitale Verwaltungsleistungen für sinnvoll.\nAuch\nMarc Danneberg\nvon\nBranchenverband Bitcom\nsah hier eine Möglichkeit, um den „Umsetzungsdruck“ zu erhöhen. Das Gesetz müsse „Planungssicherheit“ gewährleisten sowie Nutzern einen „niedrigschwelligen“ und flexiblen Zugang zu digitalen Verwaltungsleistungen ermöglichen. Es müsse auch unterschiedliche Wege eröffnen, um Zahlungen an Behörden zu leisten.\n\n„International einmaliges Gesetz“\nAnnette Guckelberger, Professorin für Öffentliches Recht an der Universität des Saarlandes,\nerklärte es für „vertretbar“, dass der Entwurf im Unterschied zum bisherigen OZG keine „pauschale Umsetzungsfrist“ enthält. Es müsse aber einen Zeitplan geben und jährliche Fortschrittsberichte, die an den Bundestag zu erstatten seien. Einen Rechtsanspruch halte sie nicht für sinnvoll, sagte Guckelberger. Digitalisierung sei ein komplexer Prozess, das OZG darin nur einer von mehreren Schritten. Auch die Länder seien gefragt, Regelungen zu beschließen.\nDas OZG sei gescheitert, stellte\nProfessor Gerhard Hammerschmid\nvon der\nBerliner Hertie-\nSchool\nfest. Es sei in seiner Zeit zwar ein „international einmaliges Gesetz“ gewesen, aber mit einem so hohen Anspruch, dass es „zum Scheitern verurteilt“ gewesen sei. Hammerschmid sprach sich gegen einen Rechtsanspruch aus, der „administrativ nicht umsetzbar“ sei, dafür umso nachdrücklicher für ein „verbindliches und transparentes“ Monitoring durch unabhängige Experten, zumal, da es künftig keine verpflichtende Umsetzungsfrist mehr geben solle.\nKompetenzen zwischen Bund und Ländern\n„Wir müssen uns gemeinsam auf die zentralen Dinge konzentrieren und das umsetzen, was schnell Nutzen bringt“, mahnte\nAlexander Handschuh\nvom\nDeutschen Städte- und Gemeindebund\n. Er wandte sich gegen die Absicht des Gesetzgebers, mit dem vorliegende Entwurf die Kommunen direkt statt allein der Länder für die Digitalisierung in die Pflicht zu nehmen. Dies schaffe „keinen Mehrwert“, ebenso wenig wie die Forderung nach einem Rechtsanspruch.\nInga Karrer\nvon der\nKoordinationsstelle\nE-Governmen\nt bei der Deutschen Industrie- und Handelsammer\ngab zu bedenken, dass eine erfolgreiche Digitalisierungsstrategie möglicherweise bedeute, die Kompetenzen zwischen Bund und Ländern im Grundgesetz neu zu verteilen. Sie erfordere ein Maß an Zentralisierung, das nach aktueller Verfassungslage nicht zulässig sei.\nSicherheit der Nutzerkonten und der Postfächer\nAls „Teil der digitalen Zivilgesellschaft“, deren Leben mit der Gesetzesnovelle „schneller und einfacher“ werden solle, stellte sich\nBianca Kastl\nvom\n\nBerliner Innovationsverbund Öffentliche Gesundheit\nvor. Sie bemängelte fehlende Klarheit und Transparenz im vorliegenden Entwurf. Dieser können „allenfalls ein Auftaktprozess einer zukunftsfähigen Verwaltung sein, die bei den Menschen ankommt, mehr leider nicht“.\n„Klare gesetzliche Vorgaben für die Sicherheit der Nutzerkonten und der Postfächer“ mahnte der Bundesdatenschutzbeauftragte Ulrich Kelber an. Auch er stellte fest, Deutschland sei „gefährlich unterdigitalisiert“. Jörg Kremer als Vertreter der Föderalen IT-Kooperation, einer in Frankfurt ansässigen Anstalt öffentlichen Rechts, die Bund und Länder bei der Verwaltungsdigitalisierung unterstützen soll, nannte das bestehende OZG „suboptimal“, aber doch zugleich eine „Initialzündung“ für digitalen Wandel. Erforderlich sei, die oftmals zu trägen Entscheidungs- und Abstimmungsprozesse in der öffentlichen Verwaltung der digitalen Welt anzupassen. Malte Spitz von Nationalen Normenkontrollrat warnte, dass auch die OZG-Novelle scheitern könnte, wenn sie nicht nachgebessert werde.\nGesetzentwurf der Bundesregierung\nMit dem Gesetzentwurf sollen die neu geschaffenen Strukturen der Bund-Länder-Zusammenarbeit verstetigt und eine einfache, moderne und digitale Verfahrensabwicklung im übergreifenden Portalverbund ermöglicht werden. Dafür will der Bund unter anderem zentrale Basisdienste bereitstellen und so landeseigene Entwicklungen für das Bürgerkonto und das Postfach ersetzen. Außerdem soll ein schriftformersetzendes qualifiziertes elektronisches Siegel und eine Regelung zu\nDigital-Only\nfür Unternehmensleistungen eingeführt werden.\nDer Nutzen des Gesetzentwurfs besteht aus Sicht der Bundesregierung darin, „dass er den Prozess der Entwicklung nutzerfreundlicher digitaler\nServices\nweiter fördert“. Dies vereinfache das Leben der Menschen und die Tätigkeit von Unternehmen und Selbständigen, soweit diese staatliche Leistungen in Anspruch nähmen.\nAntrag der Union\nDie Unionsfraktion fordert eine gesetzliche Nachfolgeregelung, die die Fortsetzung des auslaufenden Onlinezugangsgesetzes (OZG) sichert. In ihrem Antrag heißt es, es brauche einen rechtlichen, zeitlichen und finanzieller Rahmen für ein OZG 2.0, um den Ländern und Kommunen Planungssicherheit zu geben. Der IT-Planungsrat müsse vor Beginn des formalen Gesetzgebungsverfahren in ausreichender Form beteiligt werden, schreibt die Fraktion weiter. Die Bundesregierung solle zudem einen Rechtsanspruch der Bürgerinnen und Bürger ab dem 1. Januar 2025 auf die im OZG definierten Verwaltungsleistungen des Bundes vorlegen.\nIm Hinblick auf den Umsetzungsrückstand zum Fristablauf des OZG soll die Bundesregierung sich dafür einsetzen, dass „einzelne verfügbare Online-Leistungen auch flächendeckend für alle Nutzerinnen und Nutzer beschleunigt umgesetzt werden“, um eine Zersplitterung des Bereitstellungangebotes zu verhindern, fordert die Fraktion. Über eine gezielte Kommunikationsstrategie in Zusammenarbeit mit allen OZG-Dienstleistern müsse zudem der Bekanntheitsgrad und das Nutzungsverhalten von digitalen Verwaltungsdienstleistungen bei den Bürgerinnen und Bürgern unterstützt werden. (wid/joh/lbr/ste/09.10.2023)\nDokumente\n20/4313 - Antrag: Mehr Tempo bei digitaler Verwaltung - Onlinezugangsgesetz fortführen, Nutzungslücken schließen, Rechtsanspruch einführen\nPDF\n| 161 KB — Status: 08.11.2022\n(Dokument, Link öffnet ein neues Fenster)\n20/8093 - Gesetzentwurf: Entwurf eines Gesetzes zur Änderung des Onlinezugangsgesetzes sowie weiterer Vorschriften zur Digitalisierung der Verwaltung (OZG-Änderungsgesetz - OZGÄndG)\nPDF\n| 1 MB — Status: 23.08.2023\n(Dokument, Link öffnet ein neues Fenster)\nTagesordnung\n51. Sitzung am Montag, dem 9. Oktober 2023, 14.00 Uhr - Öffentliche Anhörung\n(Dokument, Link öffnet ein neues Fenster)", "excerpt_sha256": "53c58d32f2d99ea1eb18ce3aeee2b9794a97babc02304adca7debdd74c962531", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0009-E017", "source_id": "SRC-e331caeaae44d885", "url": "https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107701.html", "title": "2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Germany", "publisher": "U.S. Department of State via ecoi.net", "raw_sha256": "1745f0a1ee79ec2fc869febccc653ff096f52b71974d94ef6537e2d1e73ec7cf", "fact_summary": "The report found that Germany generally enforced laws against rape and domestic violence and supported gender-violence prevention, but shelters lacked approximately 15,000 places nationwide and had no open spaces on 303 days in 2022; it also reported persistent gender pay disparities and underfunding or insufficient independence of anti-discrimination bodies.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "6B.3", "6B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "2023 country report using 2022 service and wage data", "ruler_attribution": "German federal, state, and local institutions", "limitations": ["Some quantitative indicators predate 2023 and the report does not attribute the gaps specifically to Scholz."], "start_unit": 9, "end_unit": 11, "locator": "extracted HTML block 9 through extracted HTML block 11", "exact_excerpt": "Members of\nMarginalized or Vulnerable\nGroups\n:\nTransgender persons complained the time-consuming and costly nature of the country’s laws on gender changes limited their ability to participate in the political system (see section 6, Acts of Violence, Criminalization, and Other Abuses Based on Sexual Orientation, Gender Identity or Expression, or Sex Characteristics). They also pointed out this requirement limited the ability of transgender persons to be elected to public office, because only legal names could be used in official election records and on ballots. Persons with disabilities also faced some restrictions, although these were being reduced (see section 6, Persons with Disabilities).\nSection 4.\nCorruption in Government\nThe law provided criminal penalties for corruption by officials, and the government generally implemented the law effectively. There were isolated reports of government corruption.\nSection 5.\nGovernmental Posture Towards International and Nongovernmental Monitoring and Investigation of Alleged Abuses of Human Rights\nA variety of domestic and international human rights groups generally operated without government restriction, investigating and publishing their findings on human rights cases. Government officials were cooperative and responsive to their views.\nGovernment Human Rights Bodies\n:\nSeveral government bodies worked independently and effectively to protect human rights. The Bundestag had a Committee for Human Rights and Humanitarian Aid and a Committee for Petitions. The petitions committee fielded complaints from the public, including human rights concerns. The German Institute for Human Rights had responsibility for monitoring the country’s implementation of its international human rights commitments, including treaties and conventions.\nThe Federal Anti-Discrimination Agency (FADA) was a semi-independent body that studied discrimination and those who had been affected. The government appointed federal commissioners who advocated for the human rights of vulnerable groups, including a federal commissioner for persons with disabilities, a federal commissioner for the acceptance of sexual and gender diversity, a federal commissioner to combat antisemitism and promote Jewish life, and a federal commissioner against antiziganism (anti-Romani) and for Sinti and Roma life in Germany. The Federal Justice Ministry’s commissioner for human rights oversaw implementation of court rulings related to human rights protections.\nSection 6.\nDiscrimination and Societal Abuses\nWOMEN\nRape and Domestic Violence\n:\nThe law criminalized rape of a person, including spousal rape, regardless of gender, and provided penalties of up to 15 years in prison. Without a court order, officials could temporarily deny access to their household to those accused of abuse, or they could impose a restraining order. In cases of rape and domestic violence, authorities could prosecute individuals for assault or rape and require them to pay damages. Penalties depended on the nature of the case. The government enforced the laws effectively.\nThe federal government, the states, and NGOs supported numerous projects to prevent and respond to cases of gender-based violence, including providing survivors with greater access to medical care and legal assistance. Approximately 350 women’s shelters operated throughout the country.\nSocial service NGOs and shelter operators complained access to shelters was uneven or not available in many areas, with a shortfall in accommodation for approximately 15,000 women nationwide. According to a March report by the media outlet\nCorrectiv\n, occupancy was at 83 percent nationwide and there were no open shelter spaces on 303 days of 2022. The NGO Central Information Agency of Autonomous Women’s Shelters stated refugee women were at particular risk of domestic violence because they were required to maintain residence in a single district for three years, had few financial resources, and often resided in districts without women’s shelters.\n\nFemale Genital Mutilation/Cutting (FGM/C)\n:\nThere were no reports FGM/C was performed in the country. FGM/C of women and girls was a criminal offense punishable by one to 15 years in prison, even if performed abroad. Authorities could revoke the passports of individuals suspected of traveling abroad to subject a girl or woman to FGM/C but had not done so since the law took effect in 2017. A working group led by the Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, Women, and Youth collaborated with other federal government bodies and all 16 states to combat FGM/C.\nThe ministry continued to issue “protection letters” for girls at risk of FGM/C, warning of the high criminal penalties in the country. The letter was intended to be carried when traveling abroad and shown to relatives or others who tried to subject girls to FGM/C.\nOther\nForms of Gender-based Violence\nor Harassment\n:\nCriminal law considered so-called honor killings as murder, and the government enforced the law effectively. Although authorities estimated the number of such killings fluctuated between three and 12 during any given year, some observers questioned how many of these were so-called honor killings, which media tended to attribute to immigrant communities, and how many were other manifestations of domestic violence.\nIn February, a Berlin court sentenced two men of Afghan origin to life in prison for the July 2021 killing of their sister. Prosecutors argued the two had killed her because she had divorced her husband and was involved in a relationship with another man.\nIn October, a Berlin court convicted an Afghan man of murdering his former wife in April 2022, in what the presiding judge called a case of “classic femicide,” and sentenced him to life in prison, the harshest possible sentence. Media reported the man did not accept the divorce, which he considered an act of infidelity; the woman’s family stated she repeatedly sought police help, but police had not taken her seriously because she was an immigrant.\nThe law prohibited sexual harassment. Penalties included fines and prison sentences of up to five years. Various disciplinary measures against harassment in the workplace were available, including dismissal of the perpetrator. The law required employers to protect all employees from sexual harassment. The law considered an employer’s failure to take measures to protect employees from sexual harassment to be a breach of contract, and an affected employee had the right to paid leave until the employer rectified the problem. Unions, churches, government agencies, and NGOs operated a variety of support programs for women who experienced sexual harassment and sponsored seminars and training to prevent it.\nDiscrimination:\nMen and women enjoyed the same legal status and rights under the constitution and family, labor, religious, personal status, property, nationality, and inheritance laws. The government generally enforced the law effectively, although women did not always receive the same pay as men for equivalent work.\nThe law provided for equal pay for equal work. In January, the Federal Statistical Office found the gross hourly wages of women in 2022 were on average 18 percent lower than those of men, and 7 percent lower for women even when factors such as qualifications, responsibilities and employment biographies were equivalent. It attributed pay differences primarily to the different sectors in which men and women were employed and to unequal requirements for leadership experience and other qualifications. Women were underrepresented in highly paid managerial positions and overrepresented in some lower-wage occupations. The Federal Anti-Discrimination Agency (FADA) reported women were also at a disadvantage regarding promotions, often due to career interruptions for child rearing.\nThe law imposed a requirement of at least 30 percent women representation on the supervisory boards of certain publicly traded corporations. It also required approximately 3,500 companies to set and publish self-determined targets to increase the share of women in leading positions (executive boards and management) and to report on their performance.\nReproductive Rights:\nThere were no reports of coerced abortion or involuntary sterilization on the part of government authorities.\nWhile abortion was technically illegal in the country, existing laws protected patients, physicians, and service providers from prosecution through the 12th week under certain conditions. The law required parental permission for patients younger than age 18 to obtain an abortion, but physicians could exempt patients age 14 or older from this requirement.\nIndividuals had access to safe, effective, and affordable methods of family planning and contraception of their choice, including emergency contraception, throughout the country. Prenatal and obstetrical care was comprehensive, widely available, and covered by all insurance providers. Public information regarding contraception, reproductive health, and prenatal care was readily available free of charge from health insurance providers, NGOs, and health authorities.\nPublic health insurance covered the costs of emergency contraception and abortion in cases of rape or if medically necessary. Postexposure prophylaxis was available as part of the clinical management of rape. Emergency health care in the event of abortion complications was available and unrestricted. Emergency contraception was widely available and did not require a prescription.\nThe law required public health insurance to pay for immediate care for all survivors of sexual violence, including emergency contraception, although not all states fully implemented the law.\nSYSTEMIC RACIAL OR ETHNIC VIOLENCE AND DISCRIMINATION\nThe country’s constitution stated no one shall be “favored or disfavored because of sex, parentage, race, language, homeland and origin, faith or religious or political opinions.” Federal laws prohibited discrimination based on race or ethnicity by public authorities as well as private actors such as employers, landlords and businesses; however, the government did not always effectively enforce these laws.\nPublic incitement of hatred against an ethnic, racial, religious, or other minority was a crime, and authorities vigorously prosecuted violations of the law. Conviction of crimes motivated by such hatred also incurred harsher sentences, and judges regularly imposed these sentences.\n\nThe federal and state governments employed a wide range of measures to eliminate ethnic and racial bias. For example, in 2022, the federal government named the country’s first independent federal commissioner for antidiscrimination. The commissioner was the head of the Federal Anti-Discrimination Agency (FADA), which took complaints of discrimination and provided advice and support to victims, and could submit policy proposals, recommendations, or opinions on pending legislation to the federal government. The commissioner could also request responses from authorities to individual discrimination complaints. Some states also had similar offices. Observers noted FADA was underfunded and that both state and federal offices were not sufficiently independent. Members of minority groups were not always aware of these resources.\nThe federal and state governments also provided grants to civil society organizations working to combat racism and ethnic bias. For example, during the year the federal government program\nDemokratie Leben\n(Live Democracy) dispensed €182 million ($197 million) in grants to organizations promoting diversity and combating extremism.\nFederal and state OPCs also monitored groups with racist or xenophobic ideologies. The annual FOPC report for 2022, released in June, recorded the reporting and investigation of 20,967 politically motivated crimes committed by individuals with right-wing extremist backgrounds, a 3.8 percent increase over 2021. This included 1,016 violent crimes (a 7.5 percent increase), of which 796 were categorized as xenophobic, 16 percent more than in 2021. The 2022 FADA report listed 2,882 complaints of racism, an increase of 802 over 2021, and 1,815 complaints based on disability. The agency reported 6,627 requests for consultations from possible victims of discrimination, compared with 5,617 in 2021 and 6,383 in 2020.\nPersons of foreign origin sometimes faced difficulties finding housing. FADA reported persons not of ethnic German origin, particularly of Romani, Turkish, and African origin, including those with foreign-sounding names, were often subjected to housing discrimination. Foreign workers sometimes faced wage discrimination.\nHarassment of members of racial minorities, such as Roma and Sinti, remained a problem throughout the country. In 2022, the federal government appointed a national commissioner to combat anti-Roma prejudice and for Roma life in Germany, who coordinated government measures and advocated on behalf of the Romani community.\nThe country’s Monitoring and Information Office on Antiziganism issued its first annual report September 18, which listed 621 anti-Roma incidents in 2022, including 17 incidents of assault and one incident which it called a “very violent case”; more than half of all alleged cases involved discrimination. A fifth of the cases were tied to housing, and another fifth involved discrimination by public agencies, including employment offices, youth welfare agencies, schools, and social services offices. Commissioner to Combat Anti-Roma Prejudice and for Roma Life Mehmet Daimagueler stated “racism in job centers, police authorities and educational institutions must finally be fought decisively.” In the “very violent case,” individuals in two cars shouted anti-Roma insults at members of the Romani community in the state of Saarland, before shooting them with an air rifle, wounding several.\nCHILDREN\nChild Abuse\n:\nThere were laws against child abuse. The law punished violence, cruelty, and malicious neglect of children. Incidents of child abuse were reported. The Federal Ministry for Family, Seniors, Women, and Youth sponsored programs throughout the year on the prevention of child abuse. The ministry continued to create networks among parents, youth services, schools, pediatricians, and courts, and to support existing programs at the state and local level. Other programs provided therapy and support for young survivors of sexual abuse.\nChild,\nEarly\n,\nand\nForced Marriage\n:\nThe legal minimum age for marriage was 18 years.\nThe law nullified existing marriages conducted in other countries in which at least one spouse was younger than age 16 at the time of the wedding, even if they were of legal age in the country where the marriage was performed. Individuals ages 16 or 17 could petition a judge on a case-by-case basis to recognize their foreign marriage if they faced a specific hardship from not having their marriage legally recognized. Complete central statistics were unavailable on such cases. Child and forced marriage primarily affected girls of foreign nationality.\nSexual Exploitation of Children\n:\nThe law prohibited commercial sexual exploitation, sale, offering, or using children for commercial sexual exploitation, including child sex trafficking, as well as practices related to child pornography. Authorities enforced the law. The minimum age for consensual sex was 14 years, but sex was not considered consensual if the person was younger than age 16 and the older partner was age 18 or older and “exploiting a coercive situation.” It was illegal for adults to offer compensation for sex to a person younger than age 18. It was also illegal for a person age 21 or older to have sex with someone between ages 14 and 16 if the older person “exploits the victim’s lack of capacity for sexual self-determination.”\nThe law allowed undercover investigators to use artificially created videos of child sexual abuse to gain access to internet forums. The government’s independent commissioner for child sex abuse issues operated an online help portal and an anonymous telephone helpline free of charge.\nIn August, North Rhine-Westphalia (NRW) Minister of Interior Herbert Reul said the number of child pornography cases in NRW rose to 11,000 annually in 2021 and 2022 from 1,578 in 2013, with a successful prosecution rate of about 80 percent. Reul attributed the higher number of reported cases to better investigation and enforcement methods, and the state’s efforts to combat the problem more decisively.", "excerpt_sha256": "1cd6207e5180e08171eb2db1f892be8caa8c13173b88e1e55fe3af1dc60e7157", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0009-E019", "source_id": "SRC-e331caeaae44d885", "url": "https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107701.html", "title": "2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Germany", "publisher": "U.S. Department of State via ecoi.net", "raw_sha256": "1745f0a1ee79ec2fc869febccc653ff096f52b71974d94ef6537e2d1e73ec7cf", "fact_summary": "In June 2023, the Bundestag passed a law doubling the maximum monthly fine for employers failing to meet disability-employment quotas from 360 to 720 euros beginning in 2024, while the report found accessibility requirements for health care, public information, housing, and employment were not consistently met.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "6B.3", "6B.8", "8B.2"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Law enacted in 2023 with implementation from 2024", "ruler_attribution": "Scholz coalition and Bundestag", "limitations": ["The source does not provide evidence of the law's later enforcement effect."], "start_unit": 14, "end_unit": 14, "locator": "extracted HTML block 14", "exact_excerpt": "There were reports of medically unnecessary and irreversible “normalization” surgeries performed on intersex children or nonconsenting adults. The law and medical associations did make efforts to limit these surgeries. Advocates for intersex persons said the law included too many exceptions and needed to be strengthened.\nRestrictions of\nF\nreedom of\nE\nxpression,\nA\nssociation, or\nP\neaceful\nA\nssembly:\nThere were no laws or other restrictions on those speaking out or reporting on LGBTQI+ matters, or on the ability of LGBTQI+ individuals or organizations to legally register or convene events such as Pride festivities.\nPERSONS WITH DISABILITIES\nFederal and state laws required authorities take measures to provide for persons with disabilities to have equal treatment and access to education, health care, public buildings and services, and transportation. The law required access to information and communications, including public information in accessible formats. These requirements were not always met. For example, most physicians’ offices located in older buildings were not accessible to persons with disabilities, and there were too few health-care facilities that met the specific health care needs of persons with disabilities. Government information and communications were not always provided in accessible formats, especially at the local level.\nThe law prohibited discrimination against persons with disabilities. The law made no specific mention of the rights of persons with sensory or intellectual disabilities, but their rights were considered included under the other legal protections. NGOs disagreed on whether the government effectively enforced these provisions.\nPersons with disabilities also faced obstacles in obtaining employment and housing. Although discrimination based on a disability was illegal, there were reports of employment discrimination against persons with disabilities, and the unemployment rate among persons with disabilities of working age was much higher than in the general population. Not enough suitable employment opportunities were available for persons with disabilities, and despite requirements that private companies with more than 20 employees employ persons with disabilities, many chose to pay a monetary fine of up to €360 ($389) per month instead. In 2021, the most recent year data were available, nearly 107,000 employers did not meet their quotas, according to figures released during the year by the Federal Employment Agency. In June, the Bundestag passed a law doubling the maximum fine to €720 ($778) starting in 2024.\nThere was also a shortage of affordable, accessible, and barrier-free housing for persons with disabilities, and older and privately owned residential and commercial buildings were often exempt from accessibility regulations.\nAn estimated 1.3 million adults were living under conservatorships in the country, many of them with a disability, whose rights were restricted by various degrees under conservatorship laws. Reforms to conservatorship laws came into effect January 1, which gave persons under conservatorship more control over their own lives. NGOs such as the Institute for Human Rights stated the reforms did not go far enough. For example, the law continued to permit involuntary medical treatment or sterilization in some cases.\nState officials decided whether children with disabilities could attend mainstream or segregated schools. The law obliged all children to attend school, so those with disabilities did so at the same rate as children without disabilities. Approximately 55 percent of children with disabilities attended segregated schools, although inclusion levels varied among the country’s 16 states. Somewhat more than one-half of the students with disabilities attending mainstream schools successfully completed secondary education, compared with one in four of those attending segregated schools.\nAccording to data released in June, persons with disabilities filed 1,815 discrimination complaints with FADA in 2022, 27 percent of the total FADA received.\nOTHER SOCIETAL VIOLENCE OR DISCRIMINATION\nAccording to preliminary government figures released August 12, police registered 258 crimes directed at mosques or Muslims during the first half of the year, a 70 percent increase over the previous year.\nThe law provided for equal treatment of foreign workers, although they faced some wage discrimination. For example, employers, particularly in the construction sector, sometimes paid lower wages to seasonal workers from Eastern Europe.\nFADA stated work applicants of foreign descent with foreign names faced discrimination even when they had similar or better qualifications than others. The overall recognition rate at first instance\n     stood at 68.6% (i.e., 21.6% refugee status, 36.2% subsidiary protection and 10.9% humanitarian\n     protection). The reform\n  entered into force on 1 January 2023. The most important changes of the reform include:\n       o The introduction of independent counselling for asylum seekers, instead of the state-run\n          counselling which was introduced in 2019 (seeInformation for asylum seekers and access to\n          NGOs and UNHCR)\n       o Changes to the rules for personal interviews: an additional ground for dispensing with the\n          interview was introduced when the BAMF is of the opinion that the foreigner is unable to\n          attend a hearing due to permanent circumstances beyond their control and the possibility of\n          conducting video interviews (see Personal interview)\n       o The provisions on time limits for the asylum procedure were changed in order to closely mirror\n          the relevant provisions of the EU Asylum Procedure Directive (see Regular procedure -\n          General (scope, time limits))\n       o A change in the rules for onward appeals (Revision) to the Federal Administrative Court,\n          according to which the latter can now also adjudicate on the facts of the case, rather than\n          merely on points of law (see Regular procedure - Onward appeal).\n       o Complete revision of the grounds for cessation of the residence permit and substantial\n          amendments to the grounds of revocation of the residence permit (see Cessation and review\n          of protection status).\n  In contrast to the rules introduced by the legal reform concerning independent counselling, the\n  Highest Administrative Court decided in March 2023 that local authorities are not required to provide\n  regular access for NGOs and welfare associations to reception centres.15 Only where independent\n  counselling is explicitly requested by the applicant, must access be granted. The repercussions of\n  this and whether this leads to more difficulties in accessing legal aid need to be examined in practice.\n  In general, the introduction of independent counselling falls short of its goals due to delays in\n  implementation, practical obstacles for NGOs to access the arrival centres, insufficient funding for\n  2023 and 2024 and lack of reliable long-term funding strategies from the state (see Information for\n  asylum seekers and access to NGOs and UNHCR).\n\nv Continued reading out of mobile devices in asylum procedures: The Act to Improve Removals\n  introduced a new norm that allows the authorities to read out mobile devices. Legal scholars criticise\n  the provision as unconstitutional and the Federal Administrative Court has already emphasised that\n  it must be applied only ultima ratio (see Lodging the application).\n\nv    Increased border controls and suspected illegal push backs: The Federal Government has\n     introduced further ‘temporary’ stationary border controls on the borders with Poland, the Czech\n     Republic and Switzerland during 2023. The ruling came as a\n  result of a preliminary ruling requested by the Administrative Court of Sigmaringen in 2022, asking\n  whether the two conditions for the admissibility of a subsequent application are compatible with the\n  EU Asylum Procedures Directive (APD).18 In addition, the CJEU ruled that a temporary return to the\n  country of origin has no impact on further applications as to whether these further applications are\n  subsequent applications19 (see Subsequent applications).\n\nReception conditions\n\nv Reintroduction of emergency shelters: Due to the increasing arrivals of protection seekers,\n  several municipalities, especially larger cities, have had to reintroduce emergency shelters. The Federal Government plans to\n     provide EUR 1,3 billion in 2024 for housing to the Federal States (see Types of accommodation).\n\nv Overcrowding in arrival and reception centres: As a consequence of the increasing arrivals, many\n  regular arrival centres are heavily overcrowded. This has led to deteriorating conditions in the\n  facilities. In many municipalities there is a backlog in registrations of asylum seekers and access to\n  health care and social assistance became more difficult. Especially children do not receive adequate\n  assistance in mass accommodations. According to the Federal Government, this backlog is\n  supposedly made up for (see Conditions in reception facilities).\n\nv Integration courses: Since the beginning of 2023, all asylum seekers, no matter their nationality,\n  are now eligible to participate in integration courses.\n\nv Asylum seekers benefits (see Criteria and restrictions to access reception conditions):\n    o   The amount of benefits and its issuance in cash has been heavily debated during the course\n        of 2023. To reduce alleged ‘pull factors’, the Federal States have decided in a political\n        agreement to introduce the so-called Bezahlkarte (‘payment card’) for asylum seekers in\n        2024.20 At first glance, this card is supposed to function as any other debit card – asylum\n        seekers can pay ‘normally’ at any card payment terminal in restaurants or supermarkets.\n        However, transfers from card to card or to foreign countries should not be possible and cash\n        withdrawal is limited.21\n    o   The Act to Improve Removals entails an extension of the timeframe within which asylum\n        seekers receive only the reduced asylum seekers benefits to 36 months instead of 18\n        months.\n\n     v Access to the labour market: With the Act to Improve Removals, asylum seekers living inside\n       reception centres should already be able to work after six months, compared to nine months\n       previously. Work permits for those living outside reception centres should not be dependent upon\n       the discretion of the authorities but in all cases be allowed after three months. Asylum seekers\n       with a tolerated stay should be able to work after six months – independent of the discretion of\n       the authorities (see Access to the labour market).\n\n     v Jurisprudence on reception conditions: Following a decision by the Federal Constitutional\n       Court, single adults who live in mass accommodation centres and those who live in private\n       housing shall now receive the same amount of financial social benefits. Prior to the judgement it\n       was assumed by the authorities that those who live in mass accommodations economise together\n       and therefore require less financial benefits.22 Although the necessary corresponding legislation\n       has not been passed yet, single adults living inside and outside accommodation centres now\n       receive the same amount of asylum seekers benefits (see Forms and levels of material reception\n       conditions).\n       In February 2022 the Higher Court of Baden-Wuerttemberg ruled that private rooms in mass\n       accommodation centres are protected under the German Constitution and that consequently any\n       entry and raids by security personnel must be regulated by law and justified in the individual\n       case.23 In June 2023, however, the Federal Administrative Court restricted this right of inviolability\n       of the home again and regarded searches by security personnel as permissible.24\n\n\n20\n       Sz.de, Bezahlkarte für Flüchtlinge kommt bundesweit, 31 January 2024, https://bit.ly/42Ogpww.\n21\n       Ibid.\n22\n       Federal Constitutional Court, Decision 1 BvL 3/21, 19. More specifically:\n        o Asylum applicants can now be detained if grounds for detention applied at the time when\n            they lodged their application. Before the reform, asylum seekers could only be detained in\n            cases where they lodged the asylum request from within detention.\n        o For pre-removal detention to be ordered, it will be sufficient that the removal can take place\n            within 6 months, instead of 3.\n        o The grounds for pre-removal extension will be extended to cases where persons entered\n            legally, visa free or with a Schengen visa, and then overstayed their period for legal stay.\n        o The maximum duration of custody pending removal is to increase from 10 days to 28 days.\n        o The grounds for detention to enforce cooperation will be expanded, so that this form of\n            detention can also be orders in cases of persons who do not cooperate in the establishment\n            of their identity.\n        o Detainees who are not yet represented by a lawyer will be provided with a lawyer by the\n            court.\n        o Minors and families will not to be detained “in principle”, whereas previously they could only\n            be detained “only in exceptional cases and only for as long as it is adequate considering the\n            well-being of the child.’\n        o The new law foresees a possibility for authorities to file a complaint against the refusal by\n            courts to order detention.\n        o The Act also changes authorities’ competences for enforcing removals and tightens rules\n            around the enforcement of entry bans and extends possibilities for the expulsion of persons\n            with a criminal conviction.\n\nContent of international protection\n\nv Cessation and revocation: In 2023 the number of revocation procedures further decreased in\n  comparison to the three previous years Nevertheless, the status of 2,040 persons was revoked in\n  2023, mainly of persons from Syria, Iraq, Afghanistan and Iran (see detailed statistics under Cessation\n  and review of protection status).\n\nv    Severe cuts in funding of support measures: For 2024 the Federal government announced\n     significant cuts in the funding for services for refugees, beneficiaries for subsidiary protection and\n     other migrants. After protests and advocacy by the welfare associations providing these services,\n     the cuts were partly withdrawn (see Access to the labour market and Health care).\n\nv    Key jurisprudence on the content of international protection: In August 2022, the CJEU\n     strengthened the right to family reunification by ruling that the age cut off for family reunification\n     applied by the German authorities was contrary to EU law.27 Prior to the judgement, the child had to\n     be a minor at the time of effective reunification in cases where the sponsor was the child as well as\n     in cases where the sponsor was the parent. 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "Germany’s temporary-protection framework for people fleeing Ukraine was broader than the EU Council decision, covering Ukrainian nationals and certain family members and some third-country nationals resident in Ukraine; nevertheless, the report found that eligible third-country nationals were sometimes denied registration or pressured into asylum procedures and received less favourable access to work and benefits.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "4B.7", "6B.3", "6B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The unequal implementation and court disputes are reported during 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The framework was implemented by German federal and subnational authorities; the source does not establish Scholz’s personal direction.", "limitations": ["The report describes administrative practice and criticism but does not quantify the affected population."], "start_unit": 20, "end_unit": 21, "locator": "page 20 through page 21", "exact_excerpt": "may turn eighteen in the meantime. The CJEU decided that the age at the time of the original\n     application for international protection is decisive in both scenarios. However, this ruling only spoke\n     of family reunification of refugees. In December 2022, the Federal Administrative Court ruled that a\n     distinction between refugees and beneficiaries of subsidiary protection concerning the right to family\n     reunification does not violate the Constitution.28 The CJEU has again strengthened its ruling\n     concerning the time of the child’s minority, but its application to subsidiary protection beneficiaries by\n     German Courts remains open. In October 2023, the Federal Administrative Court ruled that\n     international protection status due to family reunification can be revoked, if the ‘principal’ person\n     entitled to international protection dies29 (see Family reunification).\n\n\nTemporary protection\n\nThe information given hereafter constitute a short summary of the German Report on Temporary\nProtection, for further information, see Annex on Temporary Protection.\n\n     v Key statistics on temporary protection: as of February 2024, 1,139,689 persons were\n       registered in the Central Register of Foreigners, and 941,559 persons held a residence permit\n       for temporary protection. As of January 2024, 65% of registered persons are women, and 30.7%\n       are under 18 years old.\n\nTemporary protection procedure\n\n     v Scope of temporary protection and disadvantageous treatment of third country nationals:\n       In Germany the scope of temporary protection is wider than in the Council decision. Temporary\n       protection is awarded to Ukrainian nationals and their family members, which includes spouses,\n       non-married partners, minor children and other close relatives if there is a ‘dependency\n       relationship’ that was already established prior to entering Germany. In 2023 however, two\n       administrative Courts expressed contradicting positions concerning non-married partners.\n       Whereas a Court in Cologne decided that a permanent partnership can be established according\n       to its exclusivity and willingness to support each other financially and emotionally, a Court in\n       Munich did not consider permanent partnership as equivalent to married couples.\n       Temporary protection may also be granted to third country nationals holding a residence permit\n       in Ukraine, even if not a permanent one, but only if they are unable to return to their countries of\n       origin. It has been criticised that in practice applicants from third countries face several\n       disadvantages in all stages of the procedure compared to Ukrainian nationals. The different\n       treatment between third country nationals applying for temporary protection and applicants of\n       Ukrainian nationality constitutes the most flagrant legal disputes as to the German\n       implementation of the Council Decision. The difference in treatment is displayed in the registration\n       process, where access to the application for temporary protection is sometimes not granted to\n       third country nationals or where eligible third country nationals are pressured into the asylum\n       procedure instead. After having lodged their application, third country nationals are further treated\n       differently with regards to access to the labour market and other social benefits. There have been\n       legal practitioners and Courts condemning this practice. However, a differentiation still takes\n       place.\n\n     v No automatic suspensive effect upon appeal: Differing from the asylum procedure, the appeal\n       of a negative decision on temporary protection does not have automatic suspensive effect, an\n       application for interim measures must be filed to guarantee legal stay during the appeal\n       proceedings.\n\n28\n       Federal Administrative Court, Voraussetzungen für den Familiennachzug zu subsidiär Schutzberechtigten,\n       press release Nr. 78/2022, 8 December 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3YcL6rO.\n29\n       Federal Administrative Court, Decision BVerwG 1 C 35.22, 11 October 2023, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/4bHhFFT.\n\n\n                                                      20\n\nv Accessibility of information on temporary protection: The German government is putting\n     effort into distributing information for people fleeing Ukraine on entry, legal stay and housing on\n     their websites in different languages.\n\n   v Flaws in identification of vulnerable groups: As for the asylum procedure no systemic\n     mechanisms exist to identify applicants with special needs and meet these needs.\n\n   v Access to regular social benefits: As of June 2022, applicants and beneficiaries of temporary\n     protection have access to regular social benefits. The unequal treatment between applicants for\n     temporary protection who receive regular social benefits and applicants for international\n     protection who receive social benefits under the Asylum Seekers Benefits Act has been criticised\n     by many civil society organisations, including in 2023.\n\nContent of temporary protection\n\n   v Excessive waiting periods for receiving residence permits: Depending on the region,\n     decision making may take several months due to a general overburdening of the local authorities.\n\n   v Obligation to reside in the allocated municipality also applies to beneficiaries of temporary\n     protection. Following legal amendments in June 2022 beneficiaries of temporary protection are\n     also obliged to reside in the municipality to which they have been allocated in the determination\n     procedure for three years. However, North Rhine-Westphalia suspended the application of this\n     obligation for beneficiaries of temporary protection.\n\n   v Dense housing situation and reintroduction of emergency shelters: While most of the\n     emergency shelters of 2016 had been dismantled, the rising numbers of people fleeing Ukraine\n     but also the continuing high numbers of people fleeing Iran, Syria and Afghanistan in combination\n     with the general lack of affordable housing led the authorities to reintroduce emergency shelters.\n     The conditions in the emergency shelters but also in overcrowded first accommodation centres\n     have been criticised by many NGOs. While beneficiaries of temporary protection are generally\n     allowed to access the regular housing market, due to the lack of affordable housing, they are\n     often required to stay in accommodation centres for extended periods. Thus, beneficiaries of\n     temporary protection are still often living in critical housing conditions, including in 2023.\n\n   v Access to regular social benefits, labour market and health care: Beneficiaries of temporary\n     protection in theory have the same access to social benefits and health care as German\n     nationals. Practical hurdles may however arise due to administrative requirements.\n\n\n\n\n                                                  21", "excerpt_sha256": "e382bce5c2bfa82f41da47e9c0acbb735c74caeb6a99b2e390ccdfa2b0b545d9", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R01-E009", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "The government distributed multilingual information on entry, legal stay, and housing for people fleeing Ukraine, but the report found no systemic mechanisms to identify applicants with special needs and described continuing administrative delays in residence-permit decisions.", "question_ids": ["6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.5", "6B.6", "8B.6"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The measures and deficiencies are described as current during the 2023 period.", "ruler_attribution": "The source attributes information distribution to the German government but does not identify Scholz’s personal action.", "limitations": ["The report does not measure the reach or effectiveness of the multilingual information."], "start_unit": 21, "end_unit": 21, "locator": "page 21", "exact_excerpt": "v Accessibility of information on temporary protection: The German government is putting\n     effort into distributing information for people fleeing Ukraine on entry, legal stay and housing on\n     their websites in different languages.\n\n   v Flaws in identification of vulnerable groups: As for the asylum procedure no systemic\n     mechanisms exist to identify applicants with special needs and meet these needs.\n\n   v Access to regular social benefits: As of June 2022, applicants and beneficiaries of temporary\n     protection have access to regular social benefits. The obtained information can only be used to establish\nidentity or country of origin, and not for other purposes during the asylum procedure.125 The types of data\nthat are used as indications for country of origin or identity are geolocation data, the countries to which\nthe majority of phone calls are made, the language of communication, the countries in which the saved\ncontacts are located, or the domain host country of frequently used websites.126\nIf an applicant refuses to hand out their smartphone, the BAMF considers the application to be withdrawn\nand ends the asylum procedure.127 For subsequent applications, failure to hand out one’s smartphone\ncan be used as a ground to withdraw material benefits, as these can be reduced for applicants who fail\nto cooperate with authorities (see Reduction or withdrawal of reception conditions). However, reading out\nof smartphones is only done in these cases when the BAMF intends to conduct an interview with the\napplicant.128\n\nThe practice of screening applicants’ smartphones was ruled illegal by the Federal Administrative Court\non 16 February 2023, after the Gesellschaft für Freiheitsrechte (GFF, an NGO focused on strategic\n\n118\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 10 March 2022.\n119\n      Information provided by the BAMF, 9 March 2023.\n120\n      For an extensive overview of data collection and management In the German asylum procedure see Janne\n      Grote, Accurate, timely, interoperable? rights) filed several lawsuits.129 The court ruled that the screening\ninterferes with the fundamental right to guarantee the confidentiality and integrity of information\ntechnology systems and that it is not lawful when less severe means are available to the BAMF to\nestablish applicants’’ identity, such as other certificates and documents (e. g. marriage certificates),\nregister comparisons and inquiries with the translator about linguistic abnormalities. Nevertheless, the\nFederal Government has passed a law for ‘improved removal’ in January 2024 that shall enable authorities\nto read out mobile devices and thus disregards the ruling of the High Court.130 The law has been heavily\ncriticised by NGOs131 as infringing upon people’s fundamental rights of privacy and informational\nautonomy.\n\nLanguage or dialect detection software can also only be used when no identity documents are provided,\nand only for applicants older than 14 years.132 Applicants have to speak into a telephone, and a speech\nanalysis software then produces a report on the languages or dialects detected and the probability that\nthese were indeed the spoken languages or dialects. As for smartphone screening, the results can never\nbe used as proof or identity or country of origin, but merely as indications which will be raised during the\npersonal interview in cases where the reports contradict the information given by the applicant. According\nto BAMF internal guidelines, as of January 2023 speech recognition is used for the main Arabic dialects\n(Maghrebian, Egyptian, Iraqi, Levantine and Gulf) and for Dari, Pashto and Persian.133 As of August 2022,\nthe detection rate for these languages and dialects was around 80% for Arabic dialects, ca. 73% for Dari\nand ca. 77% for Pashto.134 Such a speech and dialect recognition software has been used for 29,632\ncases in 2022 and for 22,947 cases from January until end of June 2023. The recognition rate for Arabic\ndialects rose to 87 % in 2023.135\n\nThe use of language detection software has been subject to criticism by NGOs and the opposition parties,\nwho claim that detection tools can be dangerous especially when asylum officers are overworked and\nthus more likely to rely on them as facts, rather than falsifiable indications. The software has been found\nto perform poorly especially for Arabic dialects in countries with a high number of local languages, such\nas Yemen or Sudan. In addition, the amount of training data for the artificial intelligence varies significantly\nbetween languages, leading to likely more accurate predictions for some languages than others.136\nFurthermore, while the BAMF has announced that a scientific study would accompany the introduction of\nthe language detection system, this has not yet happened.137\n\n\n\n\n129\n      Federal Administrative Court, Case 1 C 19.21, 16 February 2023. 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "Federal guidance on identifying vulnerable asylum seekers was not legally binding, and the federal government did not systematically monitor whether the Länder implemented it; procedures varied considerably among arrival and reception centres.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "3B.9", "4B.4", "6B.5", "8B.5"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The finding was reported in 2023 and describes the institutional position during the target year.", "ruler_attribution": "The federal government retained guidance and oversight responsibilities, while the Länder controlled much reception-centre implementation.", "limitations": ["The source does not quantify the resulting harm across the population.", "The finding concerns federal-Länder administration rather than a personal directive by Scholz."], "start_unit": 94, "end_unit": 95, "locator": "page 94 through page 95", "exact_excerpt": "guidelines. However, the guidelines are not legally binding, and the government does not systematically\nmonitor the implementation of the guidelines in the Federal states.542\n\nIn Berlin, a ‘Network for persons with special protection needs’ has developed concepts for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons and their needs since 2008. The network, which refers to itself as a\nunique project in Germany, consists of seven NGOs which cooperate with the social services of the\nregional government. The NGOs have special expertise in the support of the following groups: traumatised\npersons and victims of torture; LGBTQI+; single women and pregnant women; children and\nunaccompanied children; persons with disabilities, with chronic diseases and older persons.543 The\nnetwork was involved in the development of guidelines for the social services to assist with the\nidentification of vulnerable groups.544 The guidelines, published in August 2018, provide detailed\ninformation on how vulnerable persons can be identified and on the determination of special support\nneeds. Social services at the arrival centre Berlin are instructed to systematically screen applicants for\nvulnerability in the reception procedure. If they find that an asylum seeker has special reception needs or\nrequires special procedural guarantees, they try to take appropriate measures (including appointments\nwith specialised institutions) and inform the BAMF and the State authority accordingly.545 In spite of these\nefforts, participating NGOs of the Berlin network have reported that measures to accelerate asylum\nprocedures in the ‘arrival centre’ have had a negative impact on the identification process, since the\ninterview in the asylum procedure is often scheduled before the persons concerned have a chance to\nspeak to staff members of NGOs or of the Federal State institutions.546 In practice when an asylum seeker\nneeds special procedural guarantees, the BAMF assigns ‘special officers’ for the interview (see Special\nprocedural guarantees).547 These officers are trained and experienced decision-makers on various groups\nof vulnerable people (e.g. unaccompanied minors, victims of human trafficking, traumatized persons,\nvictims of torture, gender-specific persecution. These officers shall guarantee that the necessary\nprocedural safeguards are adhered to. NGOs have criticised the fact that special procedural needs of\nasylum seekers are not considered (i.e., the lack of support and time to prepare for an interview).548 In\naddition, identification of a vulnerability by the social services does not entail a right to specific reception\nconditions, which can still be hard to obtain especially since social services and State authorities do not\nalways work hand in hand.549\n\nEspecially since the war in Ukraine has led to rising numbers of protection seekers, systematic\nshortcomings in the identification of vulnerabilities in arrival centres have been documented.550 Despite\nthe pledge for the establishment of a Pre-screening procedure for the identification of vulnerabilities by\n\n\n542\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 20/7089, 31 May 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3SsRNpC; Bundesinitiative Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in\n      Flüchtlingsunterkünften (Federal Initiative for the protection of asylum seekers/refugees in accommodation\n      centres’), BESAFE - Besondere Schutzbedarfe bei der Aufnahme von Geflüchteten erkennen, December\n      2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Kf1jIZ.\n543\n      A list of the project partners of the „Berliner Netzwerk für besonders schutzbedürftige Flüchtlinge’ can be found\n      at: https://bit.ly/3dR5CGU.\n544\n      Leitfaden zur Identifizierung von besonders schutzbedürftigen Geflüchteten in Berlin. If the questionnaire indicates a potential vulnerability,\na screening interview takes place with the socio-psychological service of the Brandenburg immigration\nauthority (Zentrale Ausländerbehörde). Following the screening interview, if a vulnerability is detected\napplicants are referred to psychiatric counselling (which only takes place in Eisenhüttenstadt) and can be\nhoused in a special house for vulnerable applicants. The vulnerability is also communicated to the BAMF.\nHowever, in 2016 this was the case for under 1% of all asylum seekers, indicating that detection rates are\nvery low compared to the estimated prevalence of psychological distress among asylum seekers.\nFurthermore, the special accommodation houses both single men with psychological difficulties and single\nwomen who might have been victims of sexual violence.553 The Brandenburg Refugee Council criticises\nthat while there is a coordinated approach to identify vulnerabilities, the support measures vary depending\non the local governance. Accordingly, in some regions no specialised services for the accommodation of\nasylum seekers with special needs are available.554\n\nIn Lower Saxony, projects to improve the identification of vulnerable groups have been established in\nreception centres first in Friedland in 2012 and have since then expanded to all reception centres in the\nFederal State.555 Upon registration, all applicants are informed about special vulnerabilities during a\nmeeting with the social service of the reception centre and a further diagnosis is carried out in cases\nwhere there are indications of psychological disorders. While the authorities usually follow the\nrecommendations which follow from the diagnosis, between 2015 and 2017 only very few people were\nreferred for a diagnosis.556\n\nIn e.g., Rhineland-Palatinate,557 the regional government has adopted a protection concept which also\nincludes methods for the identification of vulnerabilities.558 This includes the following measures:\n     v Obligation to check for possible vulnerabilities in the reception centres during the initial stages of\n         the reception process and the asylum procedure;\n     v Intensification of communication between various actors and authorities involved in the reception\n         system and in the first steps of the asylum procedure;\n     v Documentation of possible vulnerabilities in a data system used by all authorities involved in the\n         reception process and in the asylum procedure;\n     v Training measures for persons employed by the Federal State in the reception centres to raise\n         awareness on the different forms of vulnerabilities.\n\n551\n      Berlin Senate, Senat beschließt Verfahren zur Unterstützung für besonders schutzbedürftige Geflüchtete aus\n      der Ukraine, 5 April 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48NlJCt.\n552\n      Refugee Council Berlin, Kein Ort für Schutzsuchende: Notunterkunft im Flughafen Tegel schließen, 13 March\n      2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3Ufa7Um.\n553\n      See BafF, ‘Identifizierung besonderer Schutzbedürftigkeit am Beispiel von Personen mit\n      Traumafolgestörungen. For\n1,608 of those this measure was ended due to an age assessment.575\n\n      2. Special procedural guarantees\n\n                                    Indicators: Special Procedural Guarantees\n\n      1. Are there special procedural arrangements/guarantees for vulnerable people?\n                                                           Yes        For certain categories      No\n             v If for certain categories, specify which: Unaccompanied children, traumatised persons\n                                                         Victims of torture or violence\n\n         2.1. Adequate support during the interview\n\nThe BAMF does not have specialised units dealing with vulnerable groups. According to the BAMF, all\ncase workers complete the EUAA training module on ‘Interviewing Vulnerable Persons’.576 If an applicant\nor a Federal State authority submits information to the BAMF that indicates vulnerability (such as medical\nrecords or information about specific physical, mental, intellectual or sensory impairments), such\ninformation is transferred to the case worker in charge who can decide to take measures such as allocate\nmore time for the interview, appoint an interpreter of a specific gender or allow the person to bring a\ntrusted person of their choice to the interview.577\n\nFor specific groups of vulnerable persons, the BAMF employs ‘special officers’ (Sonderbeauftragte)\nresponsible for interviews and decisions on claims by applicants with special needs. Special officers also\nadvise their colleagues in dealing with vulnerable applicants and are contact persons for specialised\ncounselling services and psycho-social centres.578 Staff members who become special officers must\ncomplete a training module for the specialisation they want to achieve. In addition, they follow the EASO\ntraining modules for their specialisation.579 Training covers both the identification and in the treatment of\nvulnerable persons.580 According to the BAMF, continuous training is offered for specific topics in the realm\nof the special officers’ responsibilities.581 The BAMF guidelines stipulate that the following cases shall be\nhandled in a particularly sensitive manner and, if necessary, by specially-trained decision-makers:582\n     v Unaccompanied children;\n     v Victims of gender-specific prosecution;\n     v Victims of human trafficking; and\n     v Victims of torture and traumatised asylum seekers.\n\nAs of June 2023, a total of 1,267 BAMF had one or more roles as special officers. This corresponds to\nroughly a third of full-time equivalent positions allocated to the first instance procedure (see Number of\n\n574\n        Nerea González Méndez de Vigo, Alterseinschätzung – ein Irrgarten ohne Ausweg? Rechtlicher Rahmen und\n        Verfahren der Alterseinschätzung in Deutschland, Asylmagazin 6-7/2019, available in German at:\n        https://bit.ly/2VI0w9G, 206-217.\n575\n        Statistisches Bundesamt, Statistiken der Kinder- und Jugendhilfe, Vorläufige Schutzmaßnahmen 2021, 27\n        July 2022, avialable in German at: https://bit.ly/3Sz6mru, 28.\n576\n        BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. The distribution among areas of responsibilities was the\nfollowing: Unaccompanied children (410), victims of gender-specific persecution (312) traumatised\npersons and victims of torture (291), victims of trafficking (254).583\n\nSpecially trained case officers may be included at all times of the asylum procedure, or take over, also\nprior to the interview, if vulnerabilitites are known. For example, if it becomes evident during the interview\nthat an asylum seeker belongs to one of these groups, the officer conducting the interview is obliged to\nconsult a special officer, in addition to notifying the reception centre if necessary and authorised by the\napplicant.584 A note on how the officers are planning to proceed must be added by the special officer to\nthe file, particularly if the special officer takes over the case as a result of their consultation. According to\ninformation provided by the government, there is an obligation in cases of unaccompanied minors for\nspecial officers to take over responsibility for the asylum procedures. In other cases of other vulnerable\ngroups, the specially trained case officer must be consulted and there are two options for further\nprocedures: either the special officer adopts an advisory role or they take over responsibility for the\nprocedure.585 However, the BAMF does not record the number of cases in which special officers are\nconsulted or in which procedures are handed over to special officers.586\n\nLawyers have reported that the introduction of special officers has led to some improvement in the\nhandling of ‘sensitive’ cases. The special officers receive special training in the area of LGBTIQ, they\nconduct interviews upon consultation by other BAMF officers and serve as multipliers for their\ncolleagues.587However, there have also been examples of cases in which indications of trauma and even\nexplicit references to torture did not lead to special officers being consulted.588 It has further been criticised\nthat there are shorcomings in the effective implementation of procedural guarantees for LGBTIQ+\npersons, which increases the risk of false decisions.589 There is no individual right to have a special officer\nhandling a person’s case, except for unaccompanied minors for whom this is mandatory. But if evidence\nsuggests that the person is vulnerable or if the person claims to have certain vulnerabilities, the interviewer\nis required to involve a special officer in the procedure, e.g. as consultant.590 However, the Administrative\nCourt of Berlin ruled that if special vulnerabilities have been detected, the absence of a special officer in\nthe asylum procedure constitutes a violation of procedural rights of vulnerable asylum seekers.591\n\n        2.2. Exemption from special procedures\n\nThe German Asylum Act exempts neither unaccompanied children nor persons with special procedural\nguarantees from the airport procedure, despite an express obligation under the recast APD to provide for\nsuch exemptions under certain conditions.592 It also makes no reference to ‘adequate support’ which\nshould be provided to those requiring special procedural guarantees.593\n\n\n\n\n583\n       The government notes that the figures cannot be added since some officers may have qualified in more than\n       one area; Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/7503, 28 June 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3Sb12cF, 2-4.\n584\n       BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. Reference source not\nfound.Subsequent applications). The number of ‘upgrade appeal’ cases and decisions remains high,\nhowever, likely as a result of long court procedures. Between January and the end of May 2023, courts\ndecided on 5.736 such appeals, and in 806 cases (14%) granted asylum or refugee protection, while in\n4,930 cases (86%) the appeal did not lead to an improvement in the protection status.753 9,525 such\nappeals of Syrian nationals were pending as of 31 May 2023, a similarly high number to the end of 2022\n(9,458).754\n\nThe removal ban for Syria that had been in place since 2012 expired at the end of December 2020. The\nban was based on a common decision of the Federal States and the Federal government, but could not\nbe renewed due to disagreement regarding the possibility to remove criminals and ‘persons posing a risk’\nrelated to terrorist activities (‘Gefährder’).This was heavily criticised by NGOs and organisations such as\nthe German Institute for Human Rights, UNHCR and Caritas.755 The removal statistics for the first half of\n2023 indicate that 410 removals of Syrian nationals took place.756 However, Syria is not listed as a country\nof destination for removals in the first half of 2023, meaning that the removals of Syrian nationals took\nplace to other countries, for example to other EU Member States in the form of Dublin transfers or\nremovals following a refusal of entry.757 As of February 2023, the Federal Government declared that it\ncurrently sees no possibilities for removals to Syria.758\n\n      2. Afghanistan\n\nEmergency evacuation since the Taliban takeover in 2021\n\nWith the takeover of the Taliban on 15 August 2021, the German government started an evacuation\noperation for German nationals in Afghanistan as well as Afghan nationals who had worked for German\nauthorities, the military and ‘especially endangered persons’. Between 16 and 26 August 2021, a total of\n5,300 persons were evacuated, out of which 4,400 Afghan nationals. The evacuated persons entered\nGermany via an emergency visa (based on Section 14 and 22 Residence Act).759 Upon arrival, the BAMF\nthen examined whether persons had already been granted permission for an admission from abroad\n(Section 22 Residence Act). If this was not the case, and if the Federal Ministry decided no such\npermission could be granted, persons were informed of this and of the possibility to apply for asylum in\nGermany. 760\n\nAfter the end of the evacuation, German authorities continued to receive and examine notifications of risk\nof former employees and of “especially endangered individuals” in exceptional circumstances. Sub-\ncontractors and consultants who worked for German authorities only indirectly are considered on a case\nby case basis according to the Federal Government. 761 If the examination confirms that the persons are\nat risk due to their work for a German authority, admission permissions continue to be granted according\n\n753\n       Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The
