and Community.762 Permissions are also included for close family\nmembers (spouses and minor siblings), other relatives are only considered in hardship cases.763 Since\ntravelling out of Afghanistan is difficult and costly, the Federal Government has provided 32 million Euros\nin funding to the GiZ (the German Development Agency) to support persons in leaving the country. It has\nbeen criticised that despite the acknowledgement that fleeing Afghanistan is difficult, there is no\nsystematic evacuation scheme.764 As a result, there have been reports that persons who managed to\ndepart from Afghanistan have been pushed back by Bulgarian, Turkish and Greek border police.765\nPersons admitted to Germany mainly leave via Pakistan and Iran. 766 Germany has also concluded an\nagreement with the government of Pakistan to allow the concerned persons to enter Pakistan with a\nTazkira K (ID card) instead of a passport as required by the Taliban to leave the country. However, in mid-\nSeptember 2022 the Federal Government reported that a total of 34 former employees and family\nmembers are known to have died in Afghanistan.767\n\nAdmission schemes\n\nGermany has been operating an admission scheme for local staff of German ministries in Afghanistan\nsince 2013. The scheme is based on Art. 22 (2) Residence Act (Temporary residence permission to uphold\nthe political interest of the Federal Republic of Germany). The eligibility criteria depend on the status of\nthe former employee. Only former staff (and their close family members) directly employed by German\nentities are covered by the programme.768\n\nFrom the takeover of the Taliban in 2021 to 17 October 2022, according to the Federal Government,\n38,100 persons had been issued a permission for admission to Germany (out of which 24,500 were former\nemployees and eligible family members, and 13,600 were especially vulnerable persons and their eligible\nfamily members). Around 26,000 of these (68.2%) persons had entered Germany up until that time. As of\n10 December 2021, a total of 28,053 permissions for admission from abroad had been issued to Afghan\nnationals and 8,014 persons had entered Germany as of the same date.769 The admission scheme for\nlocal staff continues in parallel to the new humanitarian admission scheme announced on 17 October\n2022.\n\nOn 17 October 2022, the Federal Government launched an additional federal admissions programme\nwhich had been announced in the coalition agreement of 2021.770 The government describes the\nprogramme and procedure as follows: the programme is geared towards persons who ‘have exposed\nthemselves to particular risk through their commitment to women’s and human rights or their work in the\nspheres of justice, politics, the media, education, culture, sport or academia and are thus vulnerable’ or\n‘due to the special circumstances of their individual cases have experienced or are experiencing violence\nor persecution based on their gender, sexual orientation or gender identity or religion and are therefore at\nconcrete and personal risk. In particular, these are victims of serious individual women’s rights violations,\nhomo- or transphobic human rights violations or vulnerable representatives of religious\n\n\n\n\n762\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community, Reply to written question by Canan Bayram (The Greens),\n      20/5046, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3NvUljF, 81-121.\n763\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 11.\n764\n      Taz.de, Die Mauern werden höher, 27 February 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48d59Ln.\n765\n      Ibid.\n766\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 2.\n767\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 16.\n768\n      ECRE, Afghans Seeking Protection in Europe, December 2021, available at: https://bit.ly/3krGfED, 11.\n769\n      Deutscher Bundestag, parliamentary question by The Left, 20/791, 22 February 2022, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3RPrRUC, 1.\n770\n      AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2021, April 2022, available at: https://bit.ly/3XnN7RS, 11.\n\n\n                                                      122\n\ngroups/communities.’771 The admission programme includes family members of those persons, which\nincludes spouses or same sex partners, minor children and other family members who can prove a\nrelation of dependency (beyond economic dependency) with the main person and find themselves in a\nsituation of concrete and lasting danger due to the work or vulnerability of the main person.772 The German\ngovernment appoints agencies (including civil society organisations) who can put forward names of\nsuitable persons, who must still be living in Afghanistan, via an IT application containing a questionnaire\nof a total of 41 pages.773 The names of these organisations are not made public by the government, but\naccording to a press report, PRO ASYL, Reporters without Borders, Mission Lifeline and Luftbrücke Kabul\nare taking part in the programme as of 20 December 2022.774\n\nThe Government then takes the admission decision based on selection criteria that include vulnerability\n(in line with the UNHCR catalogue of criteria), relation to Germany e. g. through language skills, family\nties, previous stays or work for German authorities or projects, level of personal exposure of the person\ne. g. through a visible / exposed position or public statements, and a special political interest on the side\nof Germany to admit a person.775 As with the previous admission programme, selected persons first\nreceive assistance to leave Afghanistan and enter a neighbouring country and are then issued a visa and\ntravel assistance by the German embassy in that country. Persons who enter Germany under the\nprogramme receive a residence permit for three years. The Federal State responsible for reception of the\npersons is to be determined according to the quota system for the distribution of asylum seekers (see\nRegistration of the asylum application), although family ties and other ‘criteria supporting integration’ are\nto be taken into account.776\n\nWhen announcing the programme, the Federal Government declared that ‘the new programme is now to\nbe implemented quickly’ and that it planned to approve around 1,000 requests per month, which is about\nthe amount of permissions granted in the months preceding the announcement. The programme is\nplanned to run until the end of the current government’s term in 2025.777 As of 30 June 2023, 229 persons\nhave been selected for admission.778 As of October 2023, only 13 of them had been admitted, due to the\npause of the admission procedure (see below).779 According to a press report, the NGOs Mission Lifeline\nand Luftbrücke Kabul alone have received around 32,000 requests as of early November 2022.780\n\nIn March 2023, the Federal Foreign office declared that all admission programs would be put to a halt for\nan indefinite time due to alleged abuse. The German newspaper Cicer and Bild published that, according\nto information they received, the admission programs had been used to bring radical Islamists to\n\n\n\n771\n      Federal Foreign Office, Joint press release by the Federal Foreign Office and the Federal Ministry of the\n      Interior and Community and Community on the federal admission programme for people from Afghanistan\n      who are at particular risk, 17 October 2022, available at: http://bit.ly/3J47ZJA.\n772\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, FAQs on the humanitarian federal\n      admission programme for Afghanistan, available at: http://bit.ly/3iVp3Xx.\n773\n      rbb.de, Aufnahmeprogramm für Afghanen startet schleppend, 20 December 2022, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/3iVdBva.\n774\n      rbb.de, Aufnahmeprogramm für Afghanen startet schleppend, 20 December 2022, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/3iVdBva.\n775\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, FAQs on the humanitarian federal\n      admission programme for Afghanistan, available at: http://bit.ly/3iVp3Xx.\n776\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, Anordnung des\n      Bundesministeriums des Innern und für Heimat gemäß § 23 Absatz 2, Absatz 3 i. A speaker of the\nForeigner’s Office declared that there were no evidence supporting a systemic misuse of the admission\nprograms.782 Only in one case a person who applied for admission has been identified as a possible\n‘threat’ (‘Gefährder’). Nevertheless, the Foreigners Office along with the Minister of Interior decided to\nintroduce additional screening mechanisms and to halt the admission program until the screening\nmechanism is in place.783 The screening procedure involves an automatic data reconciliation with\ndatabases from the national security agencies and interviews with the German domestic intelligence\nservice (Bundesamt für Verfassungsschutz), Federal Criminal Police Office (Bundeskriminalamt), the\nFederal Police or BAMF officers acting on behalf of these national security services.784 The admission\nprograms were restarted from 26 June 2023. In the first months of the newly introduced screening\nprocedure (26 June – 21 July 2023) 99 screening interviews took place, no security concerns have been\nidentified among those.785 The Federal government further states, that the capacities for the screening\nmechanism have been expanded since its start. Accordingly, as of August 2023 several hundreds of\nscreenings can be conducted per month.786\n\nThe Left party and NGOs such as PRO ASYL welcomed the launch of the programme but criticised that\n1,000 admissions per month was too low given the ‘real pressure of persecution’ for ‘people who have\nfought for democracy and human rights’. PRO ASYL further criticised that the relatively abstract selection\ncriteria could lead to an ‘ethically highly ambivalent protection lottery’787 especially in connection with the\nfact that only authorised agencies could put forward people and that the application is through an\nalgorithm-based IT application with little room to put forward specific individual circumstances.788 The\nNGO Kabul Luftbrücke reported problems with the IT application in October and November, leading to\ndelays in sending the online forms to authorities. A further point of critique is that the programme does not\nextend to persons who have managed to flee Afghanistan.789 Several NGOs also voiced concerns over\nthe practical implementation, demanding a better staffing of the counselling and coordination centres for\nthe programme and questioning the ‘organization and content’ of the procedure,790 especially given that\nit is required to have a passport in order to leave the country while obtaining one is made extremely\ndifficult by the Taliban government. One year after the official launch, in October 2023, several NGOs\nreflected on the development of the program. They demanded that the originally envisaged number of\n1,000 admissions per months should be fulfilled, that the procedure should be more transparent and that\nin additional admission schemes are necessary to meet the needs for protection.791\n\nIn addition to the Federal Government, several Federal States (Thuringia, Berlin, Hessen and Bremen)\nhave implemented admission programmes based on family ties to Afghans living in the respective Federal\nStates (for more information see Family Reunification). "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "The federal government had declared in 2021 that it would no longer pursue the AnkER-centre concept, but such centres continued to operate in practice; by September 2023 BAMF was still present in nine AnkER centres, whose operating standards were set through varying federal-state and BAMF arrangements rather than a uniform legal framework.", "question_ids": ["4B.2", "5B.6", "6B.2", "6B.4", "6B.9", "8B.2", "8B.6"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The continued operation and BAMF "locator": "page 130 through page 131", "exact_excerpt": "Reception Conditions\nShort overview of the reception system\n\nIn Germany, the Federal States are responsible for the reception of asylum seekers. Federal law provides\nthe general legal framework for reception, including the obligation to stay in an initial reception centre,\nand the amount of material benefits, while the implementation as well as more detailed regulation is the\nremit of the Federal states. In general, the Asylum Act foresees a two-stage reception procedure. Initially,\nasylum seekers are housed in initial reception centres. In a second step, and if the asylum procedure is\nnot terminated yet, asylum seekers are allocated to municipalities where they can be housed either in\ncollective accommodation centres or in a decentralised manner, in flats.\n\nAccording to the law, asylum seekers should be accommodated in an initial reception centre\n(Aufnahmeeinrichtung) for a maximum period of 18 months during the first stage of their asylum\nprocedures. Many asylum seekers do not stay in the initial reception centres for the whole 18 months,\nsince they are sent to other locations once a decision on the asylum application has been issued. As a\ngeneral exception, however, asylum seekers from safe countries of origin are obliged to stay in initial\nreception centres for the whole duration of their procedures. Furthermore, Federal States may extend the\nmaximum period to 24 months for certain groups of asylum seekers. The maximum period of stay for\nminors, their parents (or other adults entitled to custody) and their unmarried adult siblings is six months.\n\nThe initial reception centres are usually located on the same premises as the branch office of the BAMF.\nFollowing the initial reception period, most asylum seekers are sent to local accommodation centres\nwhere they have to stay for the remaining time of their procedures. The obligation to stay in such\ndecentralised accommodation centres also applies to the whole length of possible appeal procedures, but\nthere are regional differences with some municipalities also granting access to the regular housing market.\n\n‘Arrival centres’ are a form of initial reception centres set up in different locations in Germany, where\nvarious authorities are located on the same premises and where processes such as registration, identity\nchecks, the interview and the decision-making are ‘streamlined’.\n\nIn addition, ‘arrival, decision and return’ (Ankunft, Entscheidung, Rückführung, AnkER) centres were\nestablished in August 2018. The main purpose is to centralise all activities at one location and to shorten\nthe asylum procedure, which is a concept that was already applied in the ‘arrival centres’ across Germany\nand in ‘transit centres’ set up in three locations in Bavaria (Manching/Ingolstadt, Regensburg,\nDeggendorf). Initially, most Federal States did not participate in the AnkER centres scheme with only three\nFederal States (Bavaria, Saxony and Saarland) participating in the pilot project to establish AnkER\ncentres – in most cases simply by renaming their existing facilities. However, at the end of 2020, five\nadditional Federal States (Baden-Württemberg, Hamburg, Brandenburg, Mecklenburg-Vorpommern\nand Schleswig-Holstein) adjusted their reception facilities to the AnkER concept without necessarily\nusing the politically contentious name ‘AnkER centre’ for these facilities. Following the elections in 2021\nthe Federal government declared that it would not pursue the AnkER centre concept anymore. However,\nin practice the centres continue to exist. To a parliamentary request the Federal government responded\nthat accommodation facilities are run by the Federal states and that the Federal government is currently\nevaluating the cooperation with the Federal states on this issue.838 Up until September 2023, AnkER\ncentres still exist, and the BAMF is still present in nine of them.839\n\nIn any case, both arrival centres and AnkER centres are part of administrative concepts which are not\ndefined in the law and it is therefore up to the Federal States and the BAMF to define in individual\n\n\n838\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary questions by The Left 20/940, 7 March 2022, available in German\n      at: https://bit.ly/3TuNOJV.\n839\n      Bundesamt für Migration und Flüchtlinge, Ankunftszentren und AnkER-Einrichtungen, 18 September 2023,\n      available in German at: http://bit.ly/3ufkFbo.\n\n\n                                                     130\n\nagreements how these centres operate. This means that there are no general standards, but the common\nfeature is that various processes such as registration, identity checks, the interview and the decision-\nmaking are supposed to be ‘streamlined’ both in the arrival centres and the AnkER-centres. However,\nfast-tracking of procedures in this manner must not be confused with the accelerated procedure which\nwas introduced in March 2016 in the law but is not applied in practice much.\n\n\n\n A. Access and forms of reception conditions\n\n      1. Criteria and restrictions to access reception conditions\n\n                     Indicators: Criteria and Restrictions to Reception Conditions\n      1. Does the law allow for access to material reception conditions for asylum seekers in the following\n         stages of the asylum procedure?\n          v Regular procedure                         Yes     Reduced material conditions       No\n          v Dublin procedure                          Yes     Reduced material conditions       No\n          v Admissibility procedure                   Yes     Reduced material conditions       No\n          v Border procedure                          Yes     Reduced material conditions       No\n          v Accelerated procedure                     Yes     Reduced material conditions       No\n          v Appeal                                    Yes     Reduced material conditions       No\n          v Subsequent application                    Yes     Reduced material conditions       No\n\n      2. Is there a requirement in the law that only asylum seekers who lack resources are entitled to\n         material reception conditions?              Yes           No\n\nAsylum seekers are entitled to reception conditions from the moment they make their asylum application\n(Asylgesuch) in accordance with the Asylum Seekers' Benefits Act (Asylbewerberleistungsgesetz).840\nThey do not receive the full benefits, however, until they formally gain the status of an asylum seeker\nthrough the issuance of an arrival certificate (Ankunftsnachweis) at the reception centre to which they\nhave been assigned to.841 In practice, this usually happens within a few days after they have reported to\nthe authorities (see also Registration of the asylum application).842\n\nForeigners remain entitled to these reception conditions, at a minimum, as long as they have the status\nof asylum seeker. After a rejection, asylum seekers usually retain their status for the duration of the appeal\nproceedings. If the asylum application has been rejected as ‘manifestly unfounded’ or ‘inadmissible’,\nhowever, and their request for suspensive effect is rejected, asylum seekers will lose their status and will\ninstead be issued a temporary suspension of removal, also known as ‘tolerated stay’ (Duldung). In spite\nof its title, the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act does not only apply to asylum seekers, but also to people\nwith a Duldung and even to certain groups of people who have been granted a temporary residence\npermit.843\n\nIn this context, the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act does also apply to those asylum seekers whose asylum\napplication in Germany has been rejected as inadmissible and whose obligation to leave the territory is\nenforceable (‘vollziehbar ausreisepflichtig’).844 This means that the rejection is final, thus the asylum\nseeker has usually gone through an appeals process. Following the legislative reforms of August 2019,\npersons who have already been granted international protection in another EU Member State are\nexempted from this rule and should be excluded from all social benefits after a transition period of two\nweeks (see Reduction or withdrawal of reception conditions below).845\n\n\n\n\n840\n        Section 1 (1) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n841\n        Section 11 (2a) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n842\n        Section 63 (1) Asylum Act.\n843\n        Section 1 Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n844\n        Section 1 (1) Nr.4 Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n845\n        Section 1 (4) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n\n\n                                                           131", "excerpt_sha256": "785327e98caa04c3a864205076d620dfb6568e4dc764bd5afcaa2ffe5fce0479", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R04-E008", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "During 2023, the federal states decided to introduce a payment card for asylum seekers; transfers to other cards or foreign countries were intended to be blocked and cash withdrawals limited.", "question_ids": ["6B.7", "8B.2", "8B.6"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The decision and debate are reported as occurring during 2023, although the cited implementation report is dated January 2024.", "ruler_attribution": "The measure was organized by the federal states, with federal policy context; the source does not show a personal Scholz directive.", "limitations": ["The source does not establish the final nationwide implementation or measured effects during 2023."], "start_unit": 132, "end_unit": 132, "locator": "page 132", "exact_excerpt": "As a rule, asylum seekers receive both non-cash and cash financial benefits only in the town or district to\nwhich they have been assigned to.846 Accordingly, they will not be entitled to benefits in other parts of\nGermany, unless they get permission by the authorities to move there (see also Freedom of movement).\n\nThe receipt of cash (and its amount) has been heavily debated during the course of 2023. Some parties\nfind it a pull factor and want to further cut the benefits. To reduce the reputed ‘pull factors’, the Federal\nStates have decided to introduce the so-called Bezahlkarte (‘payment card’) for asylum seekers.847 Almost\nall Federal States will organise its introduction together apart from Bavaria and Mecklenburg-Western-\nPomerania, who will organise their own procedure. At first glance, this card is supposed to function as\nany other debit card – asylum seekers can pay ‘normally’ at any card payment terminal in restaurants or\nsupermarkets. However, transfers from card to card or to foreign countries should not be possible and\ncash withdrawal is limited.848\n\nAssessment of resources\n\nIf asylum seekers have an income or capital at their disposal, they are legally required to use these\nresources before they can receive benefits under the Asylum Seekers' Benefits Act.849\n\nFor example, asylum seekers are asked to hand over any cash they may possess at registration stage,\ni.e., before the application is formally lodged. The amount of money which they are allowed to keep varies\nacross the Federal states, at minimum they are allowed to keep of € 200 in cash.850 It is also possible that\nthe police carry out body searches on other occasions (e.g. when reporting to the police as asylum\nseekers, upon apprehension by the police for other reasons, or for security reasons, in reception centres)\nif they have reasons to believe that asylum seekers are in possession of documents or other information\nwhich might be essential for identification purposes. Cash that is found during such occasions is seized\nby the authorities, except for the remaining € 200 that asylum seekers are allowed to keep. The cash is\nused to compensate partially the reception costs, so asylum seekers do not get any restitution.\n\n       2. Forms and levels of material reception conditions\n\n                      Indicators: Forms and Levels of Material Reception Conditions\n      1. Amount of the monthly financial allowance/vouchers granted to asylum seekers as of 1 January\n         2024 (in original currency and in €):851\n              v Single adult in accommodation centre             € 413\n                   Single adult outside accommodation centre     € 460\n\nAssistance under the Asylum Seekers' Benefits Act generally consists of ‘basic benefits’\n(Grundleistungen). These are meant to cover the costs for food, accommodation, heating, clothing,\npersonal hygiene and consumer goods for the household (notwendiger Bedarf), as well as the personal\nneeds of everyday life, such as public transport and mobile phones (notwendiger persönlicher Bedarf)852\n– the latter is often referred to as ‘pocket money’. In addition, the necessary ‘benefits in case of illness,\npregnancy and birth’ have to be provided.853 ‘Other benefits’ can be granted in individual cases (upon\napplication) if they are necessary to safeguard the means of existence or the state of health.854\n\n\n\n846\n         Section 10 and 10a Asylum Seekers‘ Benefits Act.\n847\n         Sz.de, Bezahlkarte für Flüchtlinge kommt bundesweit, 31 January 2024, available in German at:\n         https://bit.ly/42Ogpww.\n848\n         Ibid.\n849\n         Section 7 Asylum Seekers' Benefits Act.\n850\n         Evangelischer Pressedienst (epd), Flüchtlinge müssen auch in Deutschland ihr Bargeld abgeben, 21 January\n         2016, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3QZMCuP.\n851\n         This includes hygienic items allowance and pocket money only.\n852\n         Section 3(1) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n853\n         Section 4 Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act; 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "Asylum-seeker benefits could be reduced substantially in some cases, including complete withdrawal of pocket money; reductions were limited to six months subject to review, and courts and civil-society organizations questioned aspects of the regime's constitutionality and discriminatory effects.", "question_ids": ["3B.5", "6B.3", "6B.7", "6B.8"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The legal framework was in force during 2023, though several cited court challenges predate it.", "ruler_attribution": "The rules derive from federal legislation and are administered by authorities; no personal Scholz involvement is established.", "limitations": ["The source states that reductions rarely applied during an ongoing asylum procedure and does not quantify 2023 applications of sanctions."], "start_unit": 138, "end_unit": 140, "locator": "page 138 through page 140", "exact_excerpt": "food and basic necessities, primarily as non-cash benefits. It is only ‘in special circumstances and\nindividual cases’ that further benefits can be granted on a discretionary basis.883 It has been estimated\nthat this may result in a reduction of almost 50% of the benefits in many cases.884 Benefits covering the\npersonal needs of everyday life (‘pocket money’) can be withdrawn entirely. Furthermore, asylum seekers\nare not entitled to benefits covering the costs of clothing and for ‘durable and non-durable consumer\ngoods for the household’. Clothes and household goods can only be provided ‘in kind’ and on an ad hoc\nbasis, if necessary, but these costs are not included in the monthly benefits for the persons concerned.885\n\nThe authorities are required to limit the reduction of benefits to a 6 months period. After this time, the\ndecision to reduce benefits has to be reviewed and can only be extended if the ground for reduction is\nstill applicable.886 Even before the end of the 6-months time limit, benefits have to be restored to the\nstandard level if the legal prerequisites for the reduction cease to apply. If benefits are reduced following\na rejection of an application, they can be restored to the standard level at a later stage, e.g., if a\nsubsequent application leads to the opening of a new asylum procedure, or if it turns out that a removal\nis not possible for reasons which cannot be held against the concerned person.\n\nThe decision to reduce or withdraw (see following section, Withdrawal of benefits) benefits can be\nappealed. In light of a decision of the Federal Constitutional Court of July 2012 on the Asylum Seekers’\nBenefits Act,887 there have been several court decisions concluding that any reduction of benefits would\nbe unconstitutional and therefore inadmissible, but these rulings do not represent the general opinion.888\nThe debate was revived in November 2019 by another decision of the Federal Constitutional Court. In\nthis decision, the Court did not comment on the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act, but made some important\nobservations on the legality of cuts in unemployment benefits and in the social support system in\ngeneral.889 The court argued that temporary sanctions, even to the point of a complete withdrawal of\nbenefits, could be lawful if an unemployed person did not undertake reasonable efforts to overcome the\nneed for support. However, given the extraordinary burden resulting from such sanctions, the court also\nhighlighted that legal provisions which reduce reductions of benefits have to be based on an analysis of\ntheir necessity, suitability and reasonableness. Persons affected by cuts should be able to regain standard\nbenefits once they comply with reasonable obligations. Moreover, individual circumstances must be taken\ninto consideration. Sanctions which are imposed for a fixed period of time and regardless of individual\ncircumstances have to be considered as violating the constitution, according to the Constitutional Court.\n\nAs a result of this decision, the legality of the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act has been questioned again.890\nIn several decisions, the Regional Social Court of Lower Saxony-Bremen has ruled that it is\n‘fundamentally debatable’ whether Section 1a of the Asylum Seekers Benefits Act on the reduction of\nbenefits for certain groups is in line with the constitution’.891 Other courts have also questioned the legality\n\n\n\n\n883\n      Section 1a(1) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n884\n      Joachim Genge, ‘Das geänderte Asylbeweberleistungsgesetz‘ in Informationsverbund Asyl und Migration\n      (ed), Das Migrationspaket: Beilage zum Asylmagazin 8-9/2019, September 2017, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/4aqKg1x, 20.\n885\n      Regional social administration of the Federal State of Berlin, Directive no. 10/2015, 9 December 2015\n      (Rundschreiben Soz Nr. 10/2015 über Umsetzung des AsylbLG in der Fassung des\n      Asylverfahrensbeschleunigungsgesetzes), available at: https://bit.ly/4b9xzse.\n886\n      Section 14(1) and (2) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n887\n      Federal Constitutional Court, Decision 1 BvL 10/10, 1 BvL 2/11, 18 July 2012, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/4awXg5U.\n888\n      Social Court Stade, Decision S 19 AY 19/17 ER, 10 May 2017.\n889\n      Federal Constitutional Court, Decision 1 BvL 7/16, 5 November 2019, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/41tQ7iw.\n890\n      Claudius Voigt, ‘Gesetzlich minimierte Menschenwürde. Das Sanktions-Urteil des Bundesverfassungsgerichts\n      und seine Auswirkungen auf das AsylbLG’, Asylmagazin 1-2/2020, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/4arnFSt, 12-21.\n891\n      Regional Social Court for the Federal States of Niedersachsen (Lower Saxony) and Bremen, decision of 19\n      March 2019 – L 8 AY 4/20 B ER, available in German at: http://bit.ly/40wYUyl; decision of 4 December 2019\n      -- L 8 AY 36/I9 B ER, available in German at: http://bit.ly/42DysF3.\n\n\n                                                      138\n\nof certain aspects of the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.892 However, these questions have so far only\nbeen raised in provisional proceedings in which the claimants had asked for interim measures against\ncertain sanctions. Therefore, these legal issues have only been raised but have not been decided upon\nby the courts. In any case, issues of constitutionality are a matter for the Federal Constitutional Court and\nso it has to be expected that it will take several years for suitable cases to be discussed at this level. A\nconstitutional complaint about the reduction of benefits under the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act before\nthe June 2019 amendment was rejected by the Federal Constitutional Court on the basis that in the Court\nconsidered the matter of sanctions has already sufficiently decided by prior Court rulings and that in the\nindividual case there was no breach of the Constitution. 893 In the decision of the Federal Constitutional\nCourt of October 2022 (discussed above) the court maintained its earlier rulings, that benefits may be\nmade conditional to obligations to cooperate while emphasising that any distinction between third country\nnationals and German citizens must be reasoned.894 Civil society organisations continue to argue that the\nAsylum Seekers Benefits Act is discriminatory. The debate arose again especially when in May 2022 the\nFederal government decided to award regular social benefits to temporary protection seekers (see Annex\non Temporary Protection) but not to asylum seekers.895\n\nIn practice, the reduction of benefits rarely applies to asylum seekers as long as their asylum procedure\nis ongoing. It may, however, still affect former asylum seekers whose application has been rejected as\n‘manifestly unfounded’ or ‘inadmissible’ (e.g., in cases of Dublin decisions or protection in another EU\ncountry) and in whose cases no emergency legal protection has been granted. For example, the monthly\ncash allowance (‘pocket money’) is often withdrawn or substantially reduced if the person has\n‘absconded’, i.e. failed to be present at the appointment for pick-up by the police for a ‘Dublin transfer’\n(see Dublin: Procedure). In some cases, Social Courts have argued that a reduction of benefits could be\nunlawful as long as no final decision on a possible removal (or transfer to another Dublin state) has been\nmade at the Administrative Court.896 However, such decisions are rare because only a few asylum\nseekers appeal against reductions of benefits upon rejection of their asylum application.\n\nA directive issued in the Federal State of Berlin states that minors are generally exempt from reductions\nof benefits, because the alleged misconduct cannot be held against them (e.g. if their parents have failed\nto provide the authorities with information about their identities).897 However, this policy is exceptional and\nin other Federal States it seems to be commonplace that reductions of benefits are imposed on families\nas a whole, including children.898 The former Federal Government emphasised that children are not\ngenerally exempted from sanctions.899\n\n        3.2. Withdrawal of benefits\n\nHistorically, the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act did not provide for a complete withdrawal of benefits.\nHowever, following the 2019 amendments, foreign nationals who have already been granted international\nprotection in another EU Member State are excluded from all benefits under the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits\nAct.900 Persons affected by this provision will only receive limited benefits for a maximum of two weeks\n\n\n892\n      Social Court Landshut, S 11 AY 79/19 ER, decision of 23 January 2020, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/2BQW41q; Social Court of Berlin, S 50 AY 166/19 ER, 23 December 2019, available in German\n      at: https://bit.ly/2O691an.\n893\n      Federal Constitutional Court, Case file number 1 BvR 2682/17, Decision of 12 May 2021, available in German\n      at: https://bit.ly/3GItQ5f.\n894\n      Federal Constitutional Court, Decision 1 BvL 3/21, 19 October 2022, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3z5FN2W, para 65, 75.\n895\n      Refugee Council Bavaria, ‚Menschenwürdige Sozialleistungen für alle sicherstellen!‘, 7 April 2022, available\n      at: http://bit.ly/3J8X1Tk.\n896\n      Regional Social Court Berlin-Brandenburg, Decision L 15 AY 12/17 B ER, 19 July 2017; Social Court\n      Lüneburg, Decision S 26 AY 35/17 ER, 12 September 2017.\n897\n      Regional social administration of the Federal State of Berlin, Directive no. 10/2015, 9 December 2015.\n898\n      Information provided by GGUA, Münster, 19 June 2018.\n899\n      Federal Government, Response to information request by The Left, 19/26032, 20 January 2021, available in\n      German at: http://bit.ly/3H0dJBw.\n900\n      Section 1(4) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n\n\n                                                      139\n\nand only once every two years (Überbrückungsleistungen). Further benefits may only be provided when\nnecessary ‘in exceptional circumstances’ to avoid particular hardship.901 With Berlin and Rhineland\nPalatinate, at least two Federal States have limited the scope of application of this rule to make sure the\nexclusion does not apply to minors and does not undermine the state obligation to provide a minimal\nsubsistence level of benefits.902\n\nThis exclusion applies to persons whose asylum application in Germany has been finally rejected and\nwhose obligation to leave the territory is enforceable (vollziehbar ausreisepflichtig). This can include\npersons whose appeal against a return decision is pending, if their request for suspensive effect has been\nrejected. The provision does not, however, cover situations in which a removal is impossible in fact or in\nlaw, e.g., if the Member State that has granted protection is not accepting the returnee or if necessary\nidentity documents are missing. In such cases the person affected has to be issued a Duldung and\nremains entitled to benefits under the Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n\n      4. Freedom of movement\n\n                                  Indicators: Freedom of Movement\n      1. Is there a mechanism for the dispersal of applicants across the territory of the country?\n                                                                                Yes             No\n\n      2. Does the law provide for restrictions on freedom of movement?           Yes              No\n\n         4.1. Dispersal and geographical restriction\n\nThe freedom of movement of asylum seekers is restricted and they have no right to choose their place of\nresidence. According to the Asylum Act, their right to remain on the territory under a permission to stay\n(Aufenthaltsgestattung) is generally limited to the district of the foreigners’ authority in which the\nresponsible reception centre is located.903 This ‘residence obligation’ (Residenzpflicht), legally called\n‘geographical restriction’ (räumliche Beschränkung), means that asylum seekers are not allowed to leave\nthat area even for short periods of time without permission of the BAMF. However, Federal States have\nthe possibility to extend this geographical restriction to the jurisdiction of other foreigners’ authorities or\nthe area encompassing a whole Federal State, or even to another Federal State, provided that there is\nagreement between the concerned Federal States.904 Asylum seekers in Brandenburg for example have\nthe freedom to move in all of Brandenburg and Berlin.\n\nAs long as the residence obligation applies – i.e. during the initial period of the procedure in most cases\n– the applicant can also request permission to temporary leave the assigned area for urgent public interest\nreasons, where it is necessary for compelling reasons or where refusal of permission would constitute\nundue hardship.905 As a rule, permission shall also be granted if the asylum seeker intends to take up\nemployment or education in another area. Permission shall be granted without delay in cases where the\nperson has appointments with UNHCR or NGOs.906 Next to the residence obligation, freedom of\nmovement is often constrained in practice through the remote location of many reception facilities and the\nlack of accessible public transport (see below, Obligation to stay in initial reception centres). Violation of\nthe residence obligation might have severe consequences. The administrative Court Lower Saxony-\nBreme, for example, has decided in 2023 that asylum seekers who leave the district assigned to them by\n\n\n\n\n901\n        Section 1(4) Asylum Seekers’ Benefits Act.\n902\n        Der Paritätische Gesamtverband, ‘Arbeitshilfe zum Thema Flucht und Migration. a general distribution system whereby places for\nasylum seekers are at first allocated to the Federal States for the initial reception period. Within that\nFederal State, they are allocated to a particular municipality, usually the place of the initial reception centre\nat first and possibly another municipality when the obligation to live in the initial reception centre ends.911\n\nDistribution of asylum seekers to the Federal States is determined by the following aspects:912\n\n      v Capacities of initial reception centres;\n      v Competence of the branch offices of the BAMF for the particular applicant’s country of origin. This\n        means that certain initial reception centres tend to host specific nationalities (see Differential\n        treatment of specific nationalities in reception);\n      v A quota system called ‘Königsteiner Schlüssel’,913 according to which reception capacities are\n        determined for Germany’s 16 Federal States. The Königstein key takes into account the tax\n        revenue (accounting for 2/3 of the quota) and the number of inhabitants (1/3) of each Federal State.\n\nThe quota for reception of asylum seekers More recent\nstatistics of 2023 are not yet available.\n\nIt is possible for the asylum seeker to apply to the authorities to be allocated to a particular town or district,\nbut such applications are only successful for compelling reasons (e.g. if a rare medical condition requires\nthat an asylum seeker has to stay close to a particular hospital).914 The allocation of the asylum seeker to\na particular area is not a formal decision that can be legally challenged by the individual.\n\n        4.2. Obligation to stay in initial reception centres\n\nAs a rule, asylum seekers are required to stay in the initial reception centre where they lodged their\napplication for international protection. Initial reception centres can be designated as ‘arrival centres’\n(Ankunftszentren), AnkER-centres or as separate institutions, depending on the way reception is\norganised in the Federal States. Long term stays in these centres used to be the exception. In recent\nyears, however, the obligation to stay there has been regularly extended. While the law initially foresaw\na maximum stay of 3 months, the maximum was extended to 6 months in 2015. In 2019, the German\nlegislature extended the maximum by another year – i.e., asylum seekers now may be obliged to stay in\ninitial reception centres for up to 18 months.915\n\nFor some groups of asylum seekers, the maximum obligatory stay is even longer:\n    v Asylum seekers from safe countries of origin have to stay in initial reception centres until their\n       asylum application has been decided upon and - in case of a rejection - until they leave the\n       territory.916\n    v Since 2019, under certain circumstances, asylum seekers who have failed to cooperate with the\n       authorities have to stay in initial reception centres indefinitely.917\n    v Federal States are allowed to impose an obligation on applicants to stay in initial reception centres\n       for up to 24 months.918 As of November 2021, three Federal States had regulations in place that\n       oblige asylum seekers to stay in initial reception centres for up to 24 months under Section 47(1b)\n       of the Asylum Act (see below).919\n\nHowever, the obligation to stay in initial reception centres must be limited to the duration of the first\ninstance procedure until a decision by the BAMF and may only be prolonged in case the application is\nrejected as manifestly unfounded or dismissed as inadmissible.920\n\n914\n       Stahlmann in Hofmann, Ausländerrecht §57 AsylG para. 6f., 2016.\n915\n through page 153", "exact_excerpt": "regulations provide for 6 or 7m² per person.971 A typical room in an initial reception centre has between 2\nand 4 beds, there are chairs and a table and each resident has a locker for themselves. Size of rooms\nmay vary, but rooms with a single bed are highly exceptional.\n\nMost initial reception centres have a policy to accommodate single women and families in separate\nbuildings or separate wings of their buildings. The AnKER centre in Manching/Ingolstadt for example\nprovides separate rooms for vulnerable persons.\n\nBath and toilet facilities usually consist of shower rooms and toilets which people have to share. As\nmentioned before, there are no regulations that oblige the Federal states to fulfil any standards. However,\nthe Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, Women and Youth and UNICEF have worked out\na policy paper stating minimum standards concerning the protection of refugees in reception centres.972\nThese guidelines state that sanitary facilities should be separate between genders and that those facilities\nshould be built in a way that guarantees privacy. Other guidelines recommend that one shower should be\navailable for 10 to 12 persons, but in some reception centres the ratio is worse than that, particularly in\nsituations of overcrowding. Cleaning of shared space (halls, corridors) as well as of sanitary facilities is\ncarried out by external companies in the initial reception centres.\n\nFood is supplied in the initial reception centres and is usually served in canteens on the premises of the\ncentres. In general, two or more menus are on offer for lunch and the management of the catering facilities\ntries to ensure that specific food is provided with regard to religious sentiments. Some, but not all initial\nreception centres also have shared kitchen space which enables asylum seekers to cook their own food;\nin AnkER centres, for instance, cooking is not allowed. Refrigerators for the use of asylum seekers are\navailable in some initial reception centres, but this seems to be the exception. In some centres, the\nmanagement does not allow hot water boilers for asylum seekers as this would be forbidden by fire\nregulations.973\n\nThe living conditions in many initial reception centres have been criticised by asylum seekers, volunteers\nand NGOs – especially in light of the extended obligatory stay in these facilities. In 2022 the conditions\ndeteriorated even more due to the massive overcrowding as consequence from the war in Ukraine and\nthe situation in Afghanistan. But still in 2023, small cities like Pulheim in North Rhine-Westphalia,974 as\nwell as middle size municipalities like Augsburg in Bavaria975 and Aachen in North Rhine-Westphalia976\nbut also big municipalities like Berlin977 and Hamburg978 face difficulties in accommodating new\nprotection seekers. In Berlin and Hamburg, around 97% percent of the reception capacities were\noccupied around October and November 2023.979\n\n\n\n\n971\n      European Migration Network, The Organisation of Reception Facilities for Asylum Seekers in Germany, 2013,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/48ojgO9, 26.\n972\n      Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, Women and Youth and UNICEF, Mindeststandards\n      zum Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in Flüchtlingsunterkünften, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/48WAaV2.\n973\n      PROASYL and Refugee Council Berlin, Das Asylbewerberleistungsgesetz – Einschränkungen des\n      Grundrechts auf ein menschenwürdiges Existenzminimum für Geflüchtete, November 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3XrdSox, 100.\n974\n       WDR.de, Geflüchtete klagen über menschenunwürdige Zustände in Pulheimer Flüchtlingsunterkünften,\n      available at: https://bit.ly/3SKjme1.\n975\n      Augsburger Allgemeine, Augsburg gerät bei der Unterbringung von Geflüchteten wieder stärker unter Druck,\n      19 August 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/42MZ1Iq.\n976\n      InfoMigrants, ‘Refugees: German cities are reaching their limits’, 26 September 2022, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/3wpxjCp.\n977\n      Silke Mehring, Leichtbauhallen in Tegel geplant Unterbringung von Ukraine-Flüchtlingen in Berlin wird immer\n      komplizierter, rbb24, 02 December.2022, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3wdFPUT.\n978\n      Ibid.\n979\n      Rbb24.de, Berlin droht Verlust von tausenden Unterkunftsplätzen für Geflüchtete, 28 Novermber 2023,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/48nVWzN; NDR, Flüchtlingszahlen: Senat sieht Hamburg am Limit, 6\n      October 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3SHfJ7w.\n\n\n                                                     151\n\nAccording to the administration of Berlin, 10,000 additional places were required in 2022.980 At the end\nof September 2023, the senate administration of Berlin spoke again of 8,000 more required places until\nthe end of 2023.981 Those necessary places are to be created in gymnasiums, hotels, exhibitions halls\nand through further usage of the old airport Tegel. In October 2023, the initial reception centre in Suhl,\nThuringia, was completely full and had imposed a freeze on admissions.982 However, the municipalities\ncannot offer any other accommodation facilities as all centres and apartments are full. The only possibility\nleft, according to the Thuringian Association of Towns and Municipalities, is the usage of sport halls.983\nMunicipalities in North Rine-Westphalia have started to rent hotels to provide accommodation for asylum\nseekers.984 Although this practice is very costly, it is preferred over blocking gymnasiums of local sport\nassociations. The authorities on the local, state and federal level blame each other for the shortcomings.\nWhile the local authorities are by law responsible for the accommodation of protection seekers,985 they\nclaim that the do not have enough financial and housing resources to fulfil the current need. They therefore\nask the Federal States to vacate more housing properties.986 The Federal States in turn urge the Federal\ngovernment to strengthen their efforts and to take up a coordinating role.987 After months of conflict, the\nFederal government and the Federal States have agreed in November 2023 – next to some deterrent\nmeasures that should decrease migration – upon more financial aid by the Federal Government.988\n\nAs consequence of the overcrowding, the authorities seem to be overburdened and deteriorating\nconditions have been reported. Under the law the state may derogate from the obligation to stay in initial\nreception centres in cases of overcrowding.989 Nevertheless, so far only Berlin has used this derogation\nclause and allows asylum seekers who have been allocated to Berlin under the “Köngisteiner Schlüssel”\nto live in private accommodations since the end of January 2023.990 In emergency shelters e.g. in Berlin,\nit has been reported that the tents at the former Berlin-Tegel airport do not protect from the cold causing\nnumerous illnesses and facilitating the spread of Covid-19 (on conditions in reception facilities during the\nCovid-19 pandemic see the 2021 Update).991 One partitioned area on such a tent is in general shared by\neight people. As a result, each asylum seeker has, according to calculations by the refugee council Berlin,\nonly about 2,6 m2 available for themselves.992 Additionally, since the airport is surrounded by barbed wire\nno systematic access for NGOs and volunteers is granted. At the same time protection seekers need to\ntake a shuttle bus to enter and exit the emergency shelter, thereby making it difficult for protection seekers\nto access legal aid and social assistance. In the emergency tents in Bremen protection seekers report of\nnon-functional and unclean sanitary facilities, coldness due to non-functional heating systems and a tense\natmosphere.993 Only one month later the municipality of Bremen decided to evacuate the tents due to the\n\n980\n      Thomas Frey, Ukraine-Ankunftszentrum wird um 3200 Plätze erweitert, Berliner Woche 06 January 2023,\n      available in German at: http://bit.ly/3CY9Jjy.\n981\n      Senatskanzlei Berlin, Mehr Plätze für die Unterbringung von Geflüchteten, press release from 26 September\n      2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3wnKSpd.\n982\n      Mdr.de, Erstaufnahmeeinrichtungen für Flüchtlinge – Was tun, wenn sie voll sind?, 6 October 2023, available\n      in German at: https://bit.ly/3I9iV74.\n983\n      Ibid.\n984\n      Wdr.de, Flüchtlinge in Kommunen: Letzter Ausweg Hotel, 26 January 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UPlLFY.\n985\n      §44 AsylG in conjunction with the different Federal state’s Reception laws: e.g. §1 Landesaufnahmegesetz\n      Hesse; Inhabitants were partially relocated to emergency shelters on\nexhibition grounds. This solution was only temporary and local authorities have organised lightweight\nbuilding constructions which should serve until mid-2024.994 Even if asylum seekers do not live in tents\nbut in houses, the living conditions are in many cases catastrophic. In a reception centre in Pulheim,\nNorth Rhine-Westphalia, for example, the building smells heavily, the sanitary facilities are mouldy and\nrats run around the complex.995\n\nIn arrival centres, the overcrowding mostly leads to backlogs in the registration procedure and conflicts\namong the protection seekers stemming from the lack of privacy. Asylum seekers at the arrival centre in\nHamburg-Rahlstedt, for example, have reported inter alia a backlog of registration,996 lack of privacy,\nunclean sanitary facilities and disturbances at night. The sleeping areas are placed in former warehouses\nand divided by thin partitions into several compartments, which do not allow for privacy. Besides reading\nlamps attached to each bed, there is one common light for the whole warehouse, which is switched on\nfrom 8:00am to 22:00pm.997 A backlog of registration, lack of access to health care and social assistance\nhas been reported also from the arrival centre in Berlin.998 In the arrival centre in Thuringia, many violent\nconflicts have been reported stemming from the lack of staff members, stressed social workers and non-\ntrained security personnel.999 The backlog of registrations all over Germany has supposedly been caught\nup until the end of June 2023, according to the Federal Government.1000\n\nMore generally, studies published in 2020 have come to the conclusion that the accommodation in initial\nreception centres infringes upon children’s’ rights and constitutes a danger to their mental health. The\nspatial confinement, the experience of violence and removals, as well as the permanent uncertainty cause\npsychological stress and have a negative impact on children.1001 Health care and psychosocial support\nprovided for young refugees in the mass accommodations were described as worryingly inadequate for\nmost of the facilities.1002 The study of PROASYL and the Refugee Council Berlin support these findings.\nAccording to the study of 2022, especially the access to health care, access to adequate hygienic and\nother products such as strollers for toddlers are scarce.1003\n\nThe NGO ‘Ärzte der Welt’ (Doctors of the World) announced in September 2019 that an advice service\nrun by the organisation in the AnkER-centre of Manching/Ingolstadt was to be terminated. The NGO\ndescribed living conditions in the facility as ‘morbid’ and claimed that adequate treatment, in particular\ntreatment of persons with psychological disorders, was impossible under the circumstances. Insufficient\nprotection against assaults, lack of privacy and nocturnal disturbances were impeding the mental\nstabilisation of asylum-seekers at the facility and the NGO was no longer capable to bear responsibility\nfor the mental health of its patients. Moreover, the organisation claims that there was no system for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons in place at the facility.1004\n\n994\n       Weserreport, Geflüchtete ziehen um, 10 January 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3wrtM9V.\n995\n       WDR.de, Geflüchtete klagen über menschenunwürdige Zustände in Pulheimer Flüchtlingsunterkünften,\n       available at: https://bit.ly/3SKjme1.\n996\n       Redaktionsnetzwerk Deutschland (RND), Streit in Hamburg: CDU wirft Senat unhaltbare Zustände in zentraler\n       Ankunftsstelle vor, 8 March 2022, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3XXoR9e.\n997\n       Fluchtpunkt, Mitten in Hamburg, und doch am Rand: Unzumutbare Bedingungen im Ankunftszentrum\n       Rahlstedt, November 14th 2019, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3iJgGtb.\n998\n       Taz.de, Ankunftszentrum überlaufen, 5 January 2023, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3ZZmEMf.\n999\n       MDR, Personalnot und Überforderung: Probleme in Erstaufnahmeeinrichtung nehmen zu, 29 November 2022,\n       available in German at: https://bit.ly/3GZS6B6.\n1000\n       Federal Government, response to information request by The Left, 20/8222, 05 September 2023, available in\n       German at: https://bit.ly/48E1rL3.\n1001\n       Terre des hommes, Zur Lebenssituation von minderjährigen Geflüchteten in Aufnahmeeinrichtungen, June\n       2020, available in German at: https://bit.ly/38Etah0, 7.\n1002\n       Bundesweite Arbeitsgemeinschaft der Psychosozialen Zentren für Flüchtlinge und Folteropfer (BAfF), Living\n       in a box - As a result, the regulatory\nsystem has become more restrictive and complex.\n\nPrior to March 2020, asylum seekers were barred from access to employment as long as they were under\nan obligation to stay in an initial reception centre. Outside these centres, they could be permitted to take\nup employment after having stayed in the federal territory for 3 months.\n\nAccess to employment for asylum seekers in reception centres\n\nSince March 2020, the general rule still is that asylum seekers in initial reception centres are not allowed\nto take up employment.1028 The scope of this limitation has been severely extended as the result of the\nextension of the Obligation to stay in initial reception centres. For most adult asylum-seekers, in practice\nthe time-limit before accessing employment is now 18 months, up to 24 months in some Federal States.\nNevertheless, some asylum seekers with a permission to stay (Aufenthaltsgestattung) in initial reception\ncentres are entitled to an employment permit after 9 months in the asylum procedure under certain\nconditions.1029 This applies to asylum seekers whose procedure is still ongoing before the BAMF or where\nan appeal is pending. Once their asylum procedure has been running for 9 months, they are entitled to\naccess employment pursuant to Section 61(1) of the Asylum Act if the further requirements are met.1030\nHowever, asylum seekers from safe country of origins are excluded by law from such possibilities. Hence,\nthe law establishes an unequal treatment for the latter category. Since asylum seekers from safe countries\nof origin are generally obliged to stay in initial reception centres for the whole duration of the procedure,\nthey have effective been excluded from access to the labour market.\n\nFormer asylum seekers with a tolerated stay (Duldung), who are still obliged to stay in reception centres,\nmay only be allowed to take up employment after a waiting period of 6 months from the time they are\ngranted a tolerated stay at the discretion of the authorities.1031\n\nAccess to employment for asylum seekers staying outside of reception centres\n\nOutside of reception centres, asylum seekers with a permission to stay (Aufenthaltsgestattung) are not\nallowed to take up employment during the first 3 months of their stay on the territory, after which they can\nbe permitted to do so on a discretionary basis.1032\n\nPlanned changes for 2024\n\nIn November 2023 the Federal Government drafted legislative plans to mitigate some of the restrictive\nrules.1033 Although the legislation is officially justified by relieving the burden on public budgets, these\nchanges were preceded by a prominent debate about the national lack of skilled labour. Various\nstakeholders and media reports have criticised that Germany seeks skilled labour abroad while a lot of\nasylum seekers are trained and willing to work but forbidded to do so by law.1034 After plenary discussion\nthose plans now foresee that asylum seekers inside reception centres should already be able to work\nafter six months. For those outside reception centres, working should be definitely allowed after three\nmonths and not be up to discretion of the authorities. Also, asylum seekers with a Duldung should be able\n\n1028\n       Section 61(1) 1st Sentence Asylum Act.\n1029\n       Section 61(1) 2nd Sentence Asylum Act.\n1030\n       Section 61(2) 5th Sentence Asylum Act.\n1031\n       Section 61(1) 1st Sentence Asylum Act.\n1032\n       Section 61(2) 1st Sentence Asylum Act and Section 61(2) 5th Sentence Asylum Act.\n1033\n       Deutscher Bundestag, Recommendation for a resolution and report of the Committee on Home Affairs and\n       Community (4th Committee) on the Federal Government's draft bill of the Act to Improve Removals (Entwurf\n       eines Gesetzes zur Verbesserung der Rückführung (Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz)), available in\n       German at: https://bit.ly/49CaKMs.\n1034\n       WDR.de, Fachkräfte-Mangel: Bloß keine Flüchtlinge?, 8 June 2023, available in German at:\n       https://tinyurl.com/3ak7sv67; SWR.de, Fachkräftemangel in RLP - sind Flüchtlinge die Lösung?, 16 November\n       2023, available in German at: https://tinyurl.com/ynxn83s7; Spiegel, Das große Rätsel der offenen Stellen, 17\n       March 2024, available in German at: https://tinyurl.com/2rcekmwr.\n\n\n                                                       157\n\nto work after six months without being dependent on this discretion. In November, the Federal\nGovernment has provided a draft legislation and the parliament adopted the law according to the\nrecommendation for a decision by the Ministry of Interior.1035\n\n        1.2. Restrictions on access to the labour market\n\nBefore the 2020 amendment of the Asylum Act, asylum seekers were not allowed to work on a self-\nemployed basis for the whole duration of their asylum procedure, since the permission to pursue self-\nemployment requires a regular residence permit. The asylum seeker's permission to stay\n(Aufenthaltsgestattung) does not qualify as such.1036 However, the new Section 4a(4) Residence Act now\nprovides that it is at the discretion of the responsible authorities to permit any economic activity including\nself-employment for those with a permission to stay (Aufenthaltsgestattung) or tolerated stay (Duldung).\nThis only applies to those living outside of initial reception centres, though.1037\n\nOn top of the restrictions mentioned above, there are additional limitations to the access to the labour\nmarket in practice. Firstly, asylum seekers have to apply for an employment permit each time they want\nto take up employment. To that end, they have to prove that there is a ‘concrete’ job offer, i.e. an employer\nhas to declare that the asylum seeker will be employed in case the employment permit is granted, and a\ndetailed job description must be shared with the authorities.\n\nSecondly, employment is only granted upon approval of the Federal Employment Agency.1038 There are\na few exceptions to this rule, e.g., for internships and vocational training.1039 Such approval depends inter\nalia on a ‘review of labour conditions’, i.e. an examination of whether labour rights are complied with and\nwhether wages correspond to regional standards.\n\nThe so called ‘priority review’ which was previously applied in practice, and which consisted in checking\nwhether another job-seeker would be more suited for the position (i.e. German citizens or foreigners with\na more secured residence permit) has been abandoned following the 2020 reform.\n\nThe available statistic from the Employment Agency concerning asylum seekers only encompasses data\nconcerning so-called “persons in the context of refugee migration” (Personen im Kontext von\nFluchtmigration). These are people from third countries (Drittstaatsangehörige) with either a permission\nto stay (Aufenthaltsgestattung), a permit of residence due to refugee or subsidiary status or a tolerated\nstay (Duldung). Not included in those numbers are those that migrated to Germany from Ukraine, those\nwithin family reunification with asylum seekers or those that originally came as asylum seekers but now\nhave a settlement permit (Niederlassungserlaubnis).1040 According to the statistic, 255,518 persons in the\ncontext of refugee migration are currently unemployed which means a share of 9,8% of all unemployed\npeople in Germany.\n\nAnother statistic of the Employment Agency only differentiates between those that have, inter alia,\nGerman citizenship (Deutsche) and those that do not (Ausländer). Accordingly, 16% of all people without\nGerman citizenship have been unemployed in January 2024.1041 This means a slight rise from 15,6 % in\nJanuary 2023 and from 12,6 % in January 2022. However, it has to be kept in mind that the data\n\n\n1035\n       Federal Parliament, Recommendation for a resolution and report of the Committee on Home Affairs and\n       Community (4th Committee) on the Federal Government's draft bill of the Act to Improve Removals (Entwurf\n       eines Gesetzes zur Verbesserung der Rückführung (Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz), Ds. 20/10090,\n       available in German at: https://bit.ly/3uEbG3S.\n1036\n       Section 21(6) Residence Act.\n1037\n       Section 61(1) 1st Sentence Asylum Act.\n1038\n       Section 61(1) 2nd Sentence Number 2 and Section 61(2) 1st Sentence Asylum Act.\n1039\n       Section 32(2) Employment Regulation (Beschäftigungsverordnung).\n1040\n       Statistik der Bundesagentur für Arbeit, Personen im Kontext von Fluchtmigration, 31 January 2024, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3SEIced.\n1041\n       Statistik der Bundesagentur für Arbeit, Arbeitslosigkeit im Zeitverlauf, 29 January 2024, available in German\n       at: https://tinyurl.com/3cadhrau.\n\n\n                                                       158", "excerpt_sha256": "a2c1782bb9d0f98462f79b8e8b4db0b4ddf4be2baa3aba52f9647abc655df1d5", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R05-E002", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; In 2020, only 4.1% of the persons potentially in need could be accommodated in\nPsychological Support Centres for Refugees and Victims of Torture and had to wait in average 7.2 months\nfor treatment.1103 In psychological care facilities which are not specifically trained for assisting refugees\nand victims of torture, persons in need may face language or cultural barriers which may lead to\nmisunderstandings with non-trained interpreters or psychologists.1104 The BAFF has persistently criticised\nthe German government for not meeting their obligations under international law concerning the treatment\nof asylum seekers and victims of torture. Special reception needs of vulnerable groups\n\n                                 Indicators: Special Reception Needs\n       1. Is there an assessment of special reception needs of vulnerable persons in practice?\n                                                                              Yes             No\n\nIn 2019, a provision was introduced requiring Federal States to take appropriate measures to ensure the\nprotection of women and vulnerable persons when accommodating asylum seekers in initial reception\ncentres.1108 However, this provision does not justify any legal claim for vulnerable groups concerning\nspecific measurements. The Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, Women and Youth has\npublished a handbook on minimum standards in reception centres.1109 This does also recommend\nseparate bathrooms and toilets and standardised measures to prevent gender-based violence. What\nthese ‘standardised measures’ entail is, however, not specified.\n\nEven before this provision was introduced, authorities were required to provide specific support to those\nwith special reception needs in accordance with the Reception Conditions Directive.1110 Special needs\nshould be taken into account as part of the admission procedure to the initial reception centres, and social\nworkers or medical personnel in the reception centres can assist with specific medical treatment.\nHowever, the Asylum Act does not foresee a systematic assessment procedure for vulnerable persons.\nA systematic screening for vulnerabilities is only in place in three Federal States (for details see Screening\nof vulnerability). Practices differ between Federal States and also municipalities, as not all Federal States\nhave laws or protection concepts in place that apply to all accommodation centres for asylum seekers.\nEven if concepts for protection against (gender specific) violence theoretically exist, they are not legally\n\n\n\n\n1103\n         BAfF, Bundesregierung will psychosoziale Unterstützung für traumatisierte Geflüchtete um fast 60% kürzen,\n         available in German at: https://bit.ly/3SFLBtf.\n1104\n         BafF, Traumasensibler und empowernder Umgang mit Geflüchteten ein Praxisleitfaden, 2022, available in\n         German at: https://bit.ly/48jfWDO, 36.\n1105\n         BAfF, Flucht und Gewalt, Psychosozialer Versorgungsbericht 2023, available at: https://bit.ly/49jWoQM, 11.\n1106\n         BAfF, Bundesregierung will psychosoziale Unterstützung für traumatisierte Geflüchtete um fast 60% kürzen,\n         available in German at: https://bit.ly/3SFLBtf.\n1107\n         Diakonie Deutschland, Bundeshaushalt 2024, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3UEjCwF.\n1108\n         Section 44(2a) Asylum Act.\n1109\n         Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, Women and Youth and UNICEF, Mindeststandards zum\n         Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in Flüchtlingsunterkünften, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48WAaV2.\n1110\n         Section 21 et seq. Women repeatedly report about assaults, not lockable\nsanitary facilities and confined spaces.1111\n\nThe AnkER centres and functionally equivalent reception centres usually provide for separate\naccommodation for women travelling alone and other vulnerable groups in some cases.1112 However,\nwhether or not protection of vulnerable groups is taken seriously in practice often depends on the local\nmanagement of reception centres.1113 For example, there are reports of women travelling alone being\nhoused next to men with psychological difficulties.1114\n\nBy way of example, in Rhineland-Palatinate, the regional government has adopted a protection concept\nwhich also includes methods for the identification of vulnerabilities.1115 This includes the following\nmeasures:\n    v Accommodation of possible vulnerable persons (i.e., persons who are suspected to have special\n       needs) in separate areas of the reception centres where social services can provide better care\n       and easily identify vulnerabilities;\n    v If special reception needs have been established, vulnerable persons shall be accommodated in\n       designated (i.e., separate) ‘protection areas’ with easy access to social services;\n    v If necessary, vulnerable persons shall be able to lock their rooms. Single women shall be\n       accommodated in areas to which male residents have no access and where, if possible, social\n       services and supervision are only carried out by female staff members;\n    v Separate rooms for LGBTI persons shall be provided upon request or if considered necessary by\n       the reception centre’s management staff;\n    v Persons with physical disabilities shall be accommodated in barrier-free parts of the centres and\n       shall be provided with adequate equipment. If necessary, they shall be accommodated outside of\n       the reception centres in specialised facilities for persons with disabilities.\n\nIn December 2022, the Refugee Council of Rhineland-Palatinate started a survey on how well the concept\nhas been implemented, no data is available yet.1116\n\n       1. Reception of unaccompanied children\n\nUnaccompanied children should be placed in the care of a youth welfare office which has to seek\n‘adequate accommodation’.1117 This means that, legally, unaccompanied minors are not to be placed in\ngeneral reception centres. Adequate accommodation can consist of private accommodation with other\nrelatives, at foster families, general children’s homes or specific children’s homes tailored to the need of\nforeign unaccompanied children (Clearinghäuser).1118 The type of accommodation varies according to the\ndifferent Federal States and the available capacities. Separately, a law firm reported that, among 2,458 represented clients detained pending removal between 2001 and October 2023, 1,283 cases (52.2%) were found unlawful, with affected clients detained for an average of 25.8 days.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.4", "3B.5", "3B.9", "4B.4", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The law-firm figures extend through October 2023 and the cited Supreme Court decisions include 2023 cases.", "ruler_attribution": "The evidence concerns the operation of federal detention law by local courts and authorities; it shows judicial correction but also recurring implementation failures, without establishing personal Scholz involvement.", "limitations": ["The law-firm sample is not a nationally representative dataset.", "The source reports no comprehensive nationwide statistics on judicial-review outcomes.", "Some cited case-law examples predate 2023."], "start_unit": 202, "end_unit": 204, "locator": "page 202 through page 204", "exact_excerpt": "Under German law, only a judge is competent for the order and the prolongation of detention. The\nresponsible courts are the District Courts (Amtsgericht) and their decision can be challenged at a Regional\nCourt (Landgericht), in another instance at High Regional Courts (Oberlandesgericht) and under certain\nconditions before the Federal Supreme Court (Bundesgerichtshof) as final instance.\n\nThe authorities therefore must apply to the court for a detention order. The application has to lay out the\ndetailed reasons for the necessity of detention and the authorities' entire file should be presented to the\ncourt. The foreigners should be heard by the court and shall be able to call witnesses. In cases of detention\npending removal, this may be particularly relevant if the detention order is based on an alleged risk of\nabsconding and the foreigners have to prove that they have an address at which they can be reached by\nthe authorities. Before the hearing at the court, the foreigner has to receive a copy of the request for\ndetention (Haftantrag) which the authorities have filed. This copy has to be orally translated if\nnecessary.1346 Case law also states that the foreigner shall have sufficient time to prepare an answer to\nthe content of the authorities' request. This means that it can be sufficient to hand out the request\nimmediately before the hearing if the content is simple and easily understandable. In other cases, if the\ncontent is more complicated, it can be necessary that the foreigner is handed out the authorities' request\nin advance of the hearing.1347 The court has to inform the foreigner of all possible legal remedies against\nthe detention order and this information has to be translated if necessary.\n\nDetention pending removal can only be ordered or prolonged if there is a possibility for the removal to be\ncarried out in the near future. The maximum duration of detention therefore has to be expressly stated in\nthe detention order. The immigration authority has the responsibility to monitor whether the grounds for\ndetention continue to apply and, according to administrative guidelines of the Federal Ministry of the\nInterior and Community, ‘shall immediately suspend the execution of detention for up to one week and\nimmediately apply for the revocation of the order if the grounds on which it was based no longer exist\n(62.3.3 of the General Administrative Regulation to the Residence Act)’.1348 Once the requested period of\ndetention has expired, the detained person either has to be released or an automatic judicial review of\ndetention takes place.1349\n\nIn spite of the safeguards outlined above, the system of ordering detention pending removal has been\nseverely criticised by lawyers for alleged violations of the standards applicable to detention. In particular,\nit has been noted that judges frequently issue orders for detention pending removal even if authorities’\napplications for detention orders do not lay out sufficient reasons as to why detention is necessary.1350 A\nmonitoring project on removals in North Rhine Westphalia reports that persons are frequently arrested\nand taken into detention when they come to the immigration authorities for appointments.1351 The\nConvention of Legal Advisors (Rechtsberaterkonferenz), a group of lawyers cooperating with German\nwelfare organisations on asylum matters, notes that detention pending removal is again ordered ‘too often\nand too easily’.
